# MEDIAN v0.5.0 — Sourcebook Augments Plan

## Status and authority

| Field | Current state |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Active granular plan; Part I has completed its first mapping pass. |
| **Basis** | The compressed provisional first draft of the MEDIAN v0.5.0 GDD and its Appendices. |
| **Purpose** | Plan how prose, information design, narrative, game presentations, and artwork become one designed Concept Sourcebook. |
| **Authority** | Companion production plan only. The GDD owns game design and canonical prose. This plan may place or demonstrate canon but cannot create it. |
| **Current phase** | Map Parts I–VII and Appendices A–K before returning to Part 0. |
| **Later phase** | Establish pagination, write asset briefs, produce augments, and integrate them into layouts. |

This document replaces the earlier category-only Augments skeleton. Historical
contents plans, Tale Panel plans, mockups, and artwork inventories remain idea
sources rather than current mechanical or editorial authority.

## 1. Purpose

The Augments Plan turns the complete theoretical game into a designed reading
experience. It identifies where prose needs a diagram, table, image, narrative
moment, or literal game presentation; where typography alone should carry the
page; and how the book can accumulate visual and narrative continuity without
becoming a slide deck, art portfolio, or second specification.

The plan must:

- make complex relationships easier to understand;
- let the reader imagine lived play rather than only read rules;
- establish the Corridor, Colonies, and Citizens as particular places and
  lives;
- create rhythm across a long landscape-format book;
- preserve exact reference depth in the Appendices;
- balance Mouse, Rabbit, and Squirrel civilization across the complete work;
- use recurring canon subjects so familiarity can accumulate;
- distinguish current canon from illustrative presentation; and
- produce bounded briefs that can guide layout, diagrams, mockups, writing,
  image generation, and image-to-image work.

## 2. Book-wide form

- Landscape 4:3, intended primarily for screen reading.
- The primary reading condition is one complete landscape page at a time on a
  laptop, moving vertically through the PDF. Every page therefore establishes
  its own context and completes its essential reading task without requiring
  an adjacent page to be visible.
- One unified Concept Sourcebook rather than a summary followed by a second
  Phonebook treatment.
- Every page designed; many pages intentionally contain no artwork.
- The page is the unit of composition. Prose, tables, diagrams, examples,
  narrative fragments, object cutouts, interface details, and partial
  illustration may share one page when they serve one coherent reading task.
  An augment does not own a page unless its mapping explicitly requires that
  scale.
- Continuous reading supported by strong hierarchy, running navigation,
  recurring visual grammar, and varied page density.
- Semantic text, labels, tables, diagram structure, captions, and interface
  information remain editable production elements.
- Generated imagery may provide a textless backplate or rendered scene but
  must not be the sole carrier of instructional text.
- Full-page artwork appears in two structural classes outside ordinary Chapter
  flow. **Part plates**, also called section plates during development, are
  wordless thresholds between Parts. **Chapter plates** are optional thresholds
  before selected Chapters and carry the Chapter title only.
- Text on a Chapter plate remains editable typography over a textless artwork
  source. Part plates contain no title, caption, Landscape Voice, interface
  text, or instructional label.
- Outside the Cover, End Plate, and Part 0's exceptional narrative treatment,
  only two augment classes ordinarily claim a complete page: structural artwork
  plates and full-page infographics. A full-page infographic may appear within
  a Chapter when its instructional function requires the complete page.
- The designed opening page after a wordless Part plate varies by need:
  sustained narrative, game presentation, recurring Citizen, diagram,
  typographic threshold, or silence.
- Narrative remains distributed and flexible rather than returning to a fixed
  Tale Panel format.
- Part 0 receives its own mapping pass after the regular book establishes its
  visual and narrative grammar.

Consecutive pages may answer, echo, or continue one another, and a later print
edition may reveal productive facing-page relationships. No governing
comparison, diagram, caption, reading order, or compositional subject may cross
a gutter or depend upon simultaneous spread view. In this plan, **spread** is
provisional shorthand for a short consecutive page sequence unless a record
explicitly states otherwise; pagination must convert each such sequence into
self-sufficient landscape pages.

## 3. Governing principles

### 3.1 Functional augmentation

Every augment must perform at least one defined function:

- orient the reader;
- explain a relationship;
- compare repeated fields;
- show sequence or state change;
- establish scale or spatial structure;
- demonstrate a Register or player decision;
- make a Citizen, Colony, object, or history particular;
- carry atmosphere or transition;
- support reference use; or
- create deliberate visual rest.

Decoration alone does not justify an augment. A prose page may be the fully
designed answer when another form would repeat rather than clarify.

### 3.2 One canonical owner

The GDD and its Appendices own game propositions. An augment may restate,
compare, demonstrate, or dramatize those propositions. Any new rule discovered
during augment work returns to authorial discussion and its proper GDD home
before the augment depends upon it.

### 3.3 Positive presentation

Augments show what MEDIAN is. They do not preserve removed mechanics, explain
developmental reconciliation, or retain obsolete labels merely to negate them.
Historical material can inspire form, subject, rhythm, or composition only
after its content has been checked against current canon.

### 3.4 Particularity and continuity

Narrative and visual augments prefer established Colonies, Citizens, Places,
objects, and remembered events over disposable examples. Each reappearance
respects incoming state and leaves any changed state available to later use.
Generic examples remain appropriate when particular identity would distract
from a pure comparison or formula.

### 3.5 Species balance

Rabbit carries the sustained Founding Escape. The rest of the book must answer
that prominence through strong, cumulative Mouse and Squirrel presence.
Balance is judged across the complete Sourcebook rather than page by page or
Part by Part.

### 3.6 Canon-image continuity

Existing canon imagery is mandatory input when a known Citizen or Colony is
rendered again. New composition, light, posture, activity, and emotion may
interpret the present scene; persistent body, markings, clothing, Keepsake,
Maiming, adaptive device, geography, and Colony form must follow current canon.
Appendix C and the GDD art doctrine govern reference-and-amend work.

### 3.7 Composite-page composition

Ordinary Sourcebook pages should feel authored as wholes rather than assembled
as alternating blocks of prose and pictures. A page may combine several
augment forms when hierarchy makes their relationship immediately legible:

- two or three prose measures with one partial illustration;
- a compact table or rule field nested within explanatory prose;
- object cutouts placed beside the definitions or histories they clarify;
- a wide environmental vignette sharing the page with continued reading;
- a literal game detail surrounded by editorial explanation; or
- typography, spacing, rules, and restrained background structure without art.

Composition follows function rather than a quota. One dominant element anchors
the page; supporting elements explain, particularize, compare, or provide
rest. Decorative accumulation does not substitute for hierarchy, and a useful
illustration does not require surrounding prose to vacate the page.

The visual result belongs to MEDIAN. Its typography, navigation, provisional
shared palette, highway ecology, animal-scale materiality, image treatment,
and restrained ornament govern the composition. Sourcebook precedents may
demonstrate how several elements coexist, but they do not supply MEDIAN's
fantasy language, page decoration, archetypal character treatment, or visual
identity.

Every composite page remains subject to single-page screen reading. It carries
its own heading, premise, semantic labels, and conclusion; no visual or textual
relationship depends upon an adjacent page being visible.

### 3.8 Paired narrative and system exploration

When a sequence crosses several systems or carries unusual emotional weight,
development may realize the same exact situation twice before its augment is
briefed: once as continuous original narrative and once as an explicit
mechanical state ledger. The narrative pass tests whether particular lives,
choices, place, consequence, and return cohere as experience. The simulation
pass tests whether time, Civic Balance, Stores, Carry, availability, bodily
state, and every other participating rule produce that experience without
contradiction or invisible subsidy.

Neither exploration becomes canon, tuning, or printed Sourcebook content by
existing. A useful discovery returns to authorial discussion and its canonical
home before an augment relies upon it. Use the method selectively for
high-interdependence situations rather than assigning one narrative and one
ledger to every Chapter. The Gale–Alder–Reed expedition is the first preserved
example; later candidates should earn the same depth through a real design
question.

## 4. Augment vocabulary

### 4.1 Designed prose page

A page carried by typography, hierarchy, spacing, running navigation, pull
lines, sidebars, rules, and restrained graphic structure. It is the default
page class and an affirmative design choice, not an empty slot awaiting art.

### 4.2 Table or reference panel

An exact mapping, inventory, comparison, or constraint set. Tables remain
editable, sortable by the logic of the subject, and compact enough to scan.
Reference panels isolate definitions or recurring structures without
converting every paragraph into a card.

### 4.3 System diagram

A visual account of dependency, sequence, hierarchy, geography, or state
change. It is preferred when relationships matter more than atmosphere.

### 4.4 Infographic

A substantially authored visual explanation that combines diagram, labels,
selected illustration, and concise prose. An infographic may occupy a full
page when its instructional function and information hierarchy require that
scale. Unlike a structural plate, it remains an ordinary part of Chapter flow:
its prose, labels, table structures, arrows, keys, and diagram elements stay
editable and carry the page's teaching task. Smaller infographics remain
available inside composite pages.

### 4.5 Literal game presentation

A designed representation of an actual player-facing form: DWELL, TRAVEL,
RISK, MEET, EMBODY, Almanac, Chronicle, Tale, Community Board, Citizen view,
or another established interface. It demonstrates the form's information and
experience rather than promising a final software interface.

### 4.6 Partial-page illustration

A situated image sharing the page or spread with prose. It may establish body,
material, activity, place, relationship, scale, or atmosphere while remaining
subordinate to the section's reading task.

### 4.7 Structural plates

Full-page artwork may form a structural threshold in one of two classes:

1. **Part plate (section plate).** A wordless image placed between Parts. It
   creates visual rest, announces a change of scale or subject through imagery
   alone, and leaves the next page to name the new Part and Chapter. It carries
   no title, caption, Landscape Voice, interface text, or instructional label.
2. **Chapter plate.** An optional image placed before a selected Chapter. It
   carries the Chapter title and no other text. The title is an editable
   production element laid over a textless artwork source rather than generated
   into the image.

Neither class interrupts ordinary Chapter prose. Historical full-page divider
families, including the archived **Mouse** divider, may inform the Chapter
plate's scale, restraint, and title-over-image composition after their depicted
content has been remade to current canon.

### 4.8 Narrative augment

Narrative takes the form that best serves the governing section:

1. **Sustained narrative** for a sequence whose meaning depends on continuity.
2. **Recurring lived moment** for familiarity, relationship, or ordinary life.
3. **Worked play sequence** for choice, temporal order, and causal resolution.
4. **Literal game text** for Chronicle, Tale, Almanac, MEET, or another
   player-facing narrative form.
5. **Narrative-bearing environment** for recurring geography, physical traces,
   captions, and question-bearing detail.

There is no compulsory narrative slot per Part or Chapter.

### 4.9 Landscape Voice

Landscape Voice is the human narrator describing country, weather, movement,
and simple events. It may accompany a transition, map, environmental sequence,
or the designed page following a structural plate. It does not appear on a
wordless Part plate or a title-only Chapter plate. It does not speak as a
Citizen, protagonist, Chronicle, or mechanical notification.

### 4.10 Composite page

A designed landscape page combining two or more vocabulary forms under one
reader task and visual hierarchy. Composite page is a production relationship,
not permission to add content: every ingredient retains its canonical owner,
narrative function, asset lineage, and acceptance test. It is the expected
form of many ordinary pages even when one mapped augment supplies the dominant
subject.

## 5. Mapping record

Every proposed augment receives one mapping record before production.

| Field | Required content |
|---|---|
| **Map ID** | Stable production identifier local to this plan |
| **GDD location** | Part, Chapter, subsection, Appendix, and relevant MSID |
| **Reader task** | What the reader should understand, recognize, compare, or feel |
| **Augment form** | One form from Section 4, or an approved combination |
| **Working subject** | Exact system, situation, Place, Citizen, object, or relationship |
| **Scale** | Inline, partial page, page, or spread |
| **Narrative function** | Teaching, continuity, recognition, consequence, atmosphere, or none |
| **Narrative treatment** | Sustained narrative, recurring lived moment, worked play sequence, literal game text, narrative-bearing environment, Landscape Voice, or an intentional none with its reason |
| **Register** | DWELL, TRAVEL, RISK, MEET, EMBODY, reference, or none |
| **Subjects** | Colony, Citizens, Species, counterpart, and environment |
| **Incoming state** | Facts already true before the augment begins |
| **Outgoing state** | Facts changed or established by the augment |
| **Canon inputs** | Mandatory Citizen, Colony, map, object, or visual references |
| **Semantic content** | Text, labels, data, table structure, and diagram elements that remain editable |
| **Asset lineage** | New, remake, adaptation, historical prompt, diagram, or typography-only |
| **Cross-reference** | Complementary body or Appendix treatment |
| **Priority** | Essential, valuable, or optional |
| **Approval state** | Proposed, discussed, approved, briefed, produced, integrated, or superseded |
| **Acceptance test** | The specific function by which the finished augment will be judged |

Mapping records remain concise. They specify the augment without pre-writing
final captions, prose, interface copy, or image prompts. **Scale** states the
minimum useful footprint and whether an augment must dominate; it does not
reserve the rest of a page unless the record explicitly calls for a full plate
or full-page explanatory form.

## 6. Master book map

### 6.1 Front Matter

#### Cover

- Resolve title treatment, wordmark, subtitle if any, and thumbnail-scale read.
- Choose whether the first image emphasizes the Corridor, one Colony, or the
  three Core Species together.
- Establish its relationship to the arrival plate and End Plate.

#### Reader note

- Define **Concept Sourcebook** early.
- State that the book is the complete present expression of a theoretical game,
  not a software-production promise.
- Orient the reader to body, Appendices, and navigation without front-loading
  system explanation.

#### Contents and navigation

- Establish Part, Chapter, Appendix, and MSID navigation.
- Develop running heads, page furniture, cross-reference grammar, and the
  visual distinction between main reading and reference depth.

### 6.2 Part I — What MEDIAN Is: Sanctuary and Exposure

#### Part threshold — `AUG-P1-TH-01`

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Boundary between Part 0 and Part I; supports Chapter 1.1 and `Philosophy.Pillar.AttachmentForward` |
| **Reader task** | Experience the Founding ground becoming a permanent civilization and understand base-building as accumulated Home rather than abstract expansion |
| **Augment form** | Wordless Part plate; matched transformation |
| **Working subject** | Mature New Elderbank occupying substantially the same Reach 5 ground and view established by Part 0's final arrival plate |
| **Scale** | Full page at the Part threshold |
| **Narrative function** | Temporal bridge, recognition, transformation, and visual statement of the game's premise |
| **Narrative treatment** | Narrative-bearing environment. The matched view itself tells the passage from Founding ground to inhabited Home without printed narration |
| **Register** | DWELL-adjacent environmental presentation rather than a literal interface frame |
| **Subjects** | New Elderbank and a lived population of Rabbit Citizens; exact historical moment and visible cast remain open for the asset brief |
| **Incoming state** | Three exhausted Founders overlook promising but unbuilt ground at the close of Part 0 |
| **Outgoing state** | The same overlooked ground is recognizable as a mature, inhabited Home between active highways |
| **Canon inputs** | Approved Part 0 arrival composition; current New Elderbank Colony imagery; canon references for every identifiable Citizen selected for the scene |
| **Semantic content** | None on the plate; the following designed page carries the Part and Chapter titles and any useful Landscape Voice |
| **Asset lineage** | Remake the durable “Colony Between Two Highways” concept against current New Elderbank canon |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.1, 3.1, and 6.2; Appendix H story-civilization material |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved direction; exact Tier, Citizen cast, time, weather, and camera match remain to be briefed |
| **Acceptance test** | The terrain reads as the place just reached in Part 0, the transformation reads immediately, the highway retains its magnitude, the Colony feels inhabited rather than decorative, and the plate contains no words |

**Balance consequence.** Rabbit appropriately carries this bridge from Part 0.
The next major visual assignments should lean toward Mouse and Squirrel rather
than weakening this transition through forced simultaneous representation.

No additional illustration or standalone infographic is assigned to Chapter
1.1. The typography and the preceding full plate perform the complete opening
function.

|---|---|

| **Semantic content** | **Legibility → Competence → Stability → Attention → Attachment may form**; **Home · Citizen · Place or Object · Memory**; **Attachment → Chosen Risk → Consequence → Return → Deeper Attachment**; **MEDIAN earns its cozy.** |
| **Asset lineage** | Diagrammatic and typography-only |

No additional illustration or narrative augment is assigned. The diagram
explains the proposition already made emotionally legible by Part 0 and the
matched New Elderbank threshold plate.

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 1.3 |
| **Reader task** | Hold the seven player-facing pillars together while understanding the different authority of the two governing doctrines |
| **Augment form** | Typography-led infographic using the canonical Chapter text |

The infographic is the Chapter's designed form rather than a duplicate of nine
ordinary prose subsections. It uses editable type, rules, and minimal symbols,
with no species portraits, narrative scene, or negative-definition panel.

#### 1.4 One Persistent World: Two Modes, Five Registers — `AUG-1.4-01`

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 1.4 |
| **Reader task** | Distinguish world, Mode, Register, operator, and view while understanding how one set of facts persists through every form of play |
| **Augment form** | Integrated system diagram and literal game-presentation sequence |
| **Working subject** | **One World, Two Modes, Five Registers**: formal topology paired with one Morningside expedition moving through the architecture |
| **Scale** | Two-page infographic spread |
| **Narrative function** | Mechanics teaching and visual continuity without a major new story event |
| **Narrative treatment** | Worked play sequence with recurring Squirrel subjects. Gale and one Morningside party pass through the architecture during ordinary illustrative play without creating a binding campaign incident |
| **Register** | DWELL, EMBODY, TRAVEL, RISK, and MEET |
| **Subjects** | Tier III Morningside; Gale, Alder, and Reed as the exact three-Body-Unit expedition, with Gale as the continuity anchor |
| **Incoming state** | Morningside is operating in DWELL and Gale is present and available at Home |
| **Outgoing state** | The same named party and persistent facts have moved through Launch, Away, return, and Homecoming; EMBODY is shown opening separately from restored Quiet Equilibrium |
| **Canon inputs** | Current Tier III Morningside Colony imagery; Gale's canon image and fishing-line Keepsake; Alder's and Reed's canon images and current persistent state; current Register rules |
| **Semantic content** | One persistent world-state foundation; Home with DWELL and EMBODY; Away with TRAVEL and RISK; cross-modal MEET; Launch and Homecoming transfer; one active operator; informational permeability; Register/operator/view distinction |
| **Asset lineage** | New diagrammatic structure using small literal game-presentation frames; historical “Registers” concepts may inform comparison only |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.5, 3.2, 3.8, 5.1–5.8, and 7.1 |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved direction; Gale, Alder, and Reed are fixed as the continuity party; frame count and exact illustrative state remain to be briefed |
| **Acceptance test** | The five Registers read as forms acting upon one reality, MEET visibly spans both Modes, EMBODY does not read as an expedition step, and Gale remains recognizably the same Citizen across every frame |

The spread has two layers. The first is the formal topology. The second follows
the Morningside party through DWELL selection, Launch MEET, TRAVEL, Squirrel
RISK, a brief MEET presence, return, Homecoming, and DWELL again. These are
small connected frames rather than five independent mockups; Part VII retains
the detailed presentation treatment.

#### 1.5 MEET: The Situational Decision Matrix

##### `AUG-1.5-01` — Anatomy of a Contested MEET

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 1.5 |
| **Reader task** | Understand how a specific situation, place, participants, stakes, known facts, and available responses become one legible MEET before commitment |
| **Augment form** | Literal game presentation |
| **Working subject** | A contested Node MEET involving a named Rushbottom Mouse party |
| **Scale** | One full page or spread, settled during pagination |
| **Narrative function** | Mechanics teaching through a situated illustrative example |
| **Narrative treatment** | Literal game text and a worked play frame using named Rushbottom Citizens. The response remains provisional, so the scene establishes recognition without changing their persistent state |
| **Register** | MEET arising from TRAVEL |
| **Subjects** | Four named Mouse Citizens on the player-aligned left; one non-Citizen counterpart or group on the right; exact party, Node, counterpart, and stakes remain open for the asset brief |
| **Incoming state** | The party has engaged a consequential contested Node in the Field; the known situation has crossed the MEET threshold |
| **Outgoing state** | World state remains unchanged because the shown response is provisional; the reader understands what Commit would expose to consequence |
| **Canon inputs** | Rushbottom and selected Citizen canon images; current Node, MEET, Item, Guest Signature, Focus, and presentation rules |
| **Semantic content** | Concise subject; one to three stakes; known contextual facts; **CONTEST → EVADE → PARLEY → YIELD → WITHDRAW** in fixed order; any relevant inactive family greyed with a known reason; one provisionally selected response expanded to its applicable expressions |
| **Asset lineage** | Remake the strongest scene-primary composition from the development MEET images; those images remain illustrative rather than prescriptive |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 4.6, 5.4–5.6, and 7.1; Appendix G annotated derivative |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved direction; exact situation and visible provisional expression remain to be authored |
| **Acceptance test** | The scene remains primary, party and counterpart occupy their approved sides, the response order is current, and no obsolete Health, Spirit, Unity, Fatigue, threat, success-chance, or party-portrait meter appears |

Citizens remain embodied members of the scene rather than a standing portrait
strip. Tools, Supplies, Keepsakes, relationships, and Signatures appear only
through the provisionally selected response. Focus does not appear because it
resolves only after Commit.

##### `AUG-1.5-02` — From Frame to Aftermath

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 1.5 |
| **Reader task** | Follow the temporal and causal grammar of one Round, later Rounds, state-transition MEETs, and persistent aftermath |
| **Augment form** | System diagram with a small literal Homecoming inset |
| **Working subject** | **Frame → Select → Express → Commit → Focus → Resolve → Aftermath** |
| **Scale** | One full page integrated with the Chapter's later procedural prose |
| **Narrative function** | Worked causal explanation rather than a separate story |
| **Narrative treatment** | Worked play sequence. The Homecoming inset retains the same Rushbottom party and demonstrates changed-state handling without adding a freestanding vignette |
| **Register** | MEET, with return to the owning Register |
| **Subjects** | General Round structure; the same returning Rushbottom party in the Homecoming inset |
| **Incoming state** | A MEET has framed a situation and the player has not yet committed |
| **Outgoing state** | Shared result and personal consequences have returned to their owners, or changed facts have opened another Round |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 1.5 commitment, expression, Focus, multiple-Round, resolution, and state-transition rules |
| **Semantic content** | Focus after Commit; random resolution among equal Focus claims; shared result before personal consequence; changed-state loop; four-Round maximum; return to owning Register; **Frame → Reconcile → Allocate if applicable → Continue** for state transitions |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable diagram using a small canonical Homecoming presentation rather than another full interface mockup |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 5.5 and 5.8; Appendix G |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | Selection remains reversible until Commit, expressions do not become top-level responses, Focus cannot steer choice, later Rounds preserve changed facts, and Homecoming does not masquerade as a tactical response matrix |

Together these two augments teach MEET's visible anatomy and temporal logic.
The Chapter's existing tables and prose carry participant scale, expression
classes, Choice Events, and design relationships without additional standalone
graphics.

### 6.3 Part II — The Corridor World

#### Part threshold — `AUG-P2-TH-01`

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Boundary between Parts I and II; supports Chapters 2.1 and 2.2 |
| **Reader task** | Leave the Colony-centered opening and encounter The Highway as an immense, active, inhabited landscape |

These two plates share one visual grammar but answer different spatial
questions: **what lies across the Corridor** and **how the Corridor continues**.
Each may contain several complementary diagrams, provided one dominant view
keeps the page legible.

environmental vocabulary, Founding Fit explains species-relative selection,

the world. The Chapter does not receive a separate full-page artwork for every
Biome.

#### Narrative audit — Parts I and II through Chapter 2.3

| Location | Treatment | Continuity work |
|---|---|---|
| Part I threshold | Rabbit narrative-bearing environment | Carries the Founding Escape's ground forward into mature New Elderbank |
| Chapters 1.1–1.3 | Intentional narrative rest | Lets the book name its proposition and doctrines after the sustained opening |
| Chapter 1.4 | Squirrel worked play sequence | Establishes Gale and Morningside as recurring subjects across the five Registers |
| Chapter 1.5 | Mouse literal game text and worked play | Establishes a named Rushbottom party inside MEET without forcing a binding incident |
| Part II threshold | Wordless narrative-bearing environment | Allows the Corridor itself to become the subject through image alone |
| Chapter 2.1 | Three narrative-bearing environments | Gives equal civilization weight to Mouse, Rabbit, and Squirrel without introducing disposable casts |
| Chapter 2.2 | Intentional narrative rest | Preserves the authority and reconstructability of the world's geometry |
| Chapter 2.3 | Unnamed Squirrel environmental history | Shows a Rock Cut becoming inhabited without creating a fourth Story Colony |

Part I already distributes recurring Rabbit, Squirrel, and Mouse presence.
Part II has environment and visual history but, through Chapter 2.3, no named
lived moment. Chapter 2.4 should therefore test a short Rushbottom expedition
moment as its River Spume demonstration. Chapter 2.5 can carry the stronger
Road Work sequence and the possible once-per-campaign remembrance MEET for a
Founding escapee. These are working narrative consequences to resolve with the
mechanical augments, not quotas.

The first augment explains the connected ecological world. The second narrows
that world to one distinctive phenomenon and lets it touch recognizable lives.
Other forms of human activity as weather remain in the Chapter prose and gain
their stronger temporal treatment in Chapter 2.5.

The Chapter's ordinary season, weather, World Pressure, and Civic Balance prose

locates seasonal accumulation, the traffic page shows recurring variability,
and the Road Work spread demonstrates the complete persistent event arc.

### 6.4 Part III — Home: Colony, DWELL, and EMBODY

#### Part threshold — `AUG-P3-TH-01`

The threshold answers Rabbit's Part 0–I prominence with a major Mouse civic
image. Its Citizen activity should make life legible without turning the plate
into a system key or a search puzzle.

The first augment presents the Colony as the player sees it. The second
explains the civic accounting beneath that view. Passive throughput, individual
Practices, and Project operation receive their fuller treatment in Chapters
3.4 and 3.6 rather than overloading this overview.

#### 3.3 Places and Species Placement

##### `AUG-3.3-01` — Ground Becomes Place

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|

| **Scale** | One page within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | None; this is the governing physical grammar beneath every Colony |

| **Semantic content** | Physical ground becomes a Designated Place at zero cost; a vacant designation is freely retractable and supplies no benefit; beginning the first Residence or Practice Project establishes the Place permanently; unfinished construction supplies no Capacity, strength, or output; completion activates either one Residence or one Practice; a Practice supplies the Place's functional name; the first Workshop is the sole bootstrap exception; only an explicit Guest rule permits another use relationship; later state and history remain attached to the ground |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable diagram. Manifest rows 93 and 201 may inform callout hierarchy only; their depicted Colonies, labels, uses, and relationships do not control the sequence |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 3.2, 3.4, 3.6, and 3.7; Appendix D |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | Designation is visibly free and reversible; permanence begins with the first committed construction Project rather than at completion; unfinished work provides nothing; Residence remains a Place use rather than a Practice; one-use-per-Place remains the rule; the first Workshop is the only bootstrap exception; and later development changes a particular Place rather than raising a generic level |

##### `AUG-3.3-02` — Where a Place Belongs

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 3.3, Species placement and Spatial Alignment |

| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 3.3 Species-placement and Spatial-Alignment rules; current Residence Capacity and passive-placement bonus; the approved illustrative maxim |

Rabbit builds a Cul-de-Sac. Squirrel builds a Web.**; Well Placed and Not Well Placed as judgments of relationships rather than skins; Residence Capacity increasing from two to three Body Units when Well Placed; Practice situated strength; an additional half-yield where the Practice defines passive production; Citizen support remaining mandatory; one small later-state inset showing development improve or degrade alignment, such as an obscured Rabbit common center |
| **Asset lineage** | Complete current-spec remake of manifest row 104's three-species comparison. Rows 93, 103, 150, 151, 165, 166, and 201 may inform comparative or callout hierarchy only; none supplies current rules or a reusable Colony plan |

| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | All three Species receive equal visual weight; the same civic uses remain recognizable across all fields; spatial relationship rather than decoration determines alignment; no field becomes a fixed blueprint; the half-yield never bypasses required Role support; later change remains legible; and the page does not imply that one Place may ordinarily contain several Practices |

Together the diagram and comparison separate two questions that should never be
collapsed: **how a Place becomes a lasting physical fact** and **how that fact
belongs within a Species-shaped Colony**. Chapter 3.4 can therefore begin with
the civic work performed through those Places rather than repeating their
construction or placement rules.

##### `AUG-3.4-03` — Work That Continues / Work That Changes

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Closing of Chapter 3.4, Shared Practices and Project Queue |
| **Reader task** | Distinguish ordinary Role contribution from a committed Project and follow the complete Project Queue operation without confusing supporting Practice, target Place, or owning Role |
| **Augment form** | Two-page editable system diagram with one anonymous worked example |
| **Working subject** | **Work That Continues / Work That Changes** |
| **Scale** | One two-page spread at the Chapter close |
| **Narrative function** | Worked civic process rather than a named story event |
| **Narrative treatment** | Worked play sequence without a recurring cast. An unnamed Builder establishing a Garden makes the distinction among Citizen, owning Role, supporting Workshop, and target Place concrete without fixing a Story Citizen's Role history |
| **Register** | DWELL, with DAWN recording shown as the relevant clock boundary |
| **Subjects** | Ordinary Role support; compatible completed Practices; one active Project slot per usable Practice; named committed Citizens; material reservation; Project target; Civic Share-Days; DAWN; completion and return |
| **Incoming state** | The Role entries have established which responsibility owns each lasting result |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can preview the civic cost of commitment, distinguish support from target, follow progress to completion, and understand how deliberate change temporarily reduces ordinary coverage |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 3.4 Shared Practices and Project Queue; Chapter 3.2 Civic Share and commitment rules; current first-Workshop exception |
| **Semantic content** | Left field: available Citizens sustain ordinary Role coverage, compatible Practice Strength, and any defined passive work. Right field: choose a persistent result, supporting Practice, material, and named Citizens of the owning Role; preview lost Role coverage, Practice support, and passive throughput; Commit occupies one Project slot and withdraws those Civic Shares; Civic Share-Days accumulate; DAWN records whole progress; committed work cannot be cancelled or interrupted; completion makes the result true, releases the Practice, and returns Citizens to ordinary availability. Worked example: a Workshop supports Builder Citizens establishing a Garden at another Place. Supporting Practice and target remain distinct. Builder's first Workshop is the sole bootstrap exception. One restrained boundary callout distinguishes ordinary Wound recovery—Patient Load plus Hearth occupancy—from the Queue, while retaining Rehabilitation as a persistent Caretaker Project |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable diagram and typography; no historical queue mockup controls its form |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 3.2, 3.3, 3.6, 4.6, 5.7, and 6.4; Appendix D |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | Practices provide capacity but perform no labor; every Project has named committed Citizens; the owning Role follows the intended result; supporting Practice and target may differ; opportunity cost is visible before Commit; fractional progress is not awarded before DAWN records a whole amount; cancellation and interruption are absent; Wound recovery never appears as a Project; and the diagram does not reproduce the passive-production formula reserved for Chapter 3.6 |

These three treatments keep the Chapter's hierarchy legible: the opening map
supplies the whole civic vocabulary, the entry architecture preserves seven
distinct responsibilities, and the closing spread explains how ordinary work
becomes deliberate lasting change. Passive-production arithmetic remains in
Chapter 3.6, where production and transformation can be treated together.

Road Work is marked as the defined non-absorbable exception and referred to Chapter 2.5 |

##### `AUG-3.5-02` — Water at Rushbottom

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 3.5, Telegraph, Choosing through Home MEET, and Consequence and aftermath |
| **Reader task** | Experience the general Pressure grammar as one recognizable Home moves from warning through preparation into a situated choice |
| **Augment form** | Two-page worked play sequence combining a restrained DWELL preparation field with one literal Home MEET presentation |
| **Working subject** | **Water at Rushbottom** |
| **Scale** | One two-page spread within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | Mouse continuity, recognition of threatened ordinary life, and concrete demonstration of preparation and choice |
| **Narrative treatment** | Worked play sequence and recurring environment. Tier III Rushbottom returns after its ordinary Part-threshold appearance; Sump and selected recognizable Mouse Citizens inhabit the situation without every visible Citizen acquiring a fixed mechanical part |
| **Register** | DWELL, then Home MEET |
| **Subjects** | Rushbottom's Garden and part of its Joined House; Sump; selected Rushbottom Mice; heavy runoff; the Almanac; applicable Civic Shares, Practices, and response expressions |
| **Incoming state** | Rushbottom is an inhabited Tier III Home; Sump is an established Toad Guest; an eligible approaching wet-ground condition becomes known early enough to permit limited preparation |
| **Outgoing state** | The MEET reaches commitment with its exact selected response and persistent aftermath deliberately left for the later asset brief; the sequence establishes that any consequence will remain attached to Rushbottom's named lives and ground |
| **Canon inputs** | Current Tier III Rushbottom identity and imagery; canon images for Sump and every identifiable Mouse; Chapter 3.5 Home-Pressure choice grammar; Toad **Wet-Ground Care**; current Almanac and Community Board rules |
| **Semantic content** | First beat: qualitative Telegraph enters the Almanac without an exact chance or countdown. Second beat: DWELL permits the player to reassign uncommitted Civic Shares or adjust Provisioning while time and ordinary commitments remain real. Third beat: heavy runoff threatens the Garden as primary stake and the Joined House as collateral stake. The Home MEET presents the Pressure-specific choices to protect the primary stake, protect the collateral stake instead, or endure the combined consequence; cost, protected value, and likely direction remain legible before Commit. **Wet-Ground Care** opens a Caretaker expression within the eligible Garden response rather than becoming a fourth top-level option or substituting for Gardener responsibility. Exact commitment, personal consequence, physical aftermath, and any following Project remain open for the asset brief |
| **Asset lineage** | Remake the strongest Rushbottom storm composition from manifest rows 202 and 204. Preserve recognizable water pressure, Colony ground, and animal-scale urgency while rebuilding the exact Rushbottom state, Citizens, Garden, Residence, interface, choices, and generated text from current canon |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.5, 3.2, 3.4, 4.3, and 7.1; Appendices F and G |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved direction; selected Mouse cast, exact Telegraph, committed response, and persistent aftermath remain to be briefed |
| **Acceptance test** | The threatened ground is recognizable as the Rushbottom already encountered; Telegraph creates a bounded preparation window rather than certainty; the three Pressure responses remain top-level; Sump changes one eligible expression without replacing a Core Role; immediate stakes are legible before commitment; the situation is neither a defense mode nor an invisible Colony-wide damage roll; and unresolved aftermath is not silently invented during mapping |

The first augment explains why a situation becomes worthy of attention. The
second lets the reader inhabit that transition without pretending the hidden
selection process is a visible optimization puzzle. Its open outcome is a
deliberate asset-brief decision, not missing system logic.

#### 3.6 Resources, Production, and Provisioning

##### `AUG-3.6-01` — One Rule, Four Processes

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 3.6, Resources through Provisioning |
| **Reader task** | Understand the Colony's resource vocabulary, the shared passive-throughput rule, the four defined processes, and the controls that prevent automatic transformation from consuming everything available |

| **Working subject** | **One Rule, Four Processes** |
| **Scale** | One two-page spread within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | None; this is the precise economy grammar used by many later situations |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. The preceding Rushbottom sequence has supplied a particular Home under pressure; this spread gives its resources and transformations an exact, general account |
| **Register** | DWELL reference, with DAWN identified as the award boundary |
| **Subjects** | Perishable Sustenance, Durable Sustenance, Flexible Scrap, Rigid Scrap, Supplies, Tools; Cultivation, Preservation, Supply Preparation, and Remedy Preparation; Prepare, Hold, and Unavailable |
| **Incoming state** | Chapter 3.4 has established compatible Roles and Practices; Chapter 3.5 has shown those resources and strengths entering a situated response |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can calculate ordinary passive throughput, identify every gate and later bound, understand fractional accumulation, and distinguish passive transformation from deliberate Tool creation |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 3.6 Resources, Production and transformation, and Provisioning; Appendix D passive-production reference; current Supply and Tool definitions |
| **Semantic content** | Six concise resource definitions; three aligned process rows: Garden plus Gardener produces Perishable Sustenance; Workshop plus Crafter prepares Binding, Device, and Offering Supplies; Storehouse plus Caretaker creates one shared Provisioning throughput allocated between Perishable-to-Durable Preservation and Remedy Preparation. A process produces zero without at least one usable relevant Practice and one Citizen sustaining its Role. Once open: **Throughput = usable relevant Practices + sustaining Civic Shares + (0.5 × Well-Placed relevant Practices)**. Each additional Practice and sustaining Share contributes additively; Project-committed Citizens contribute nothing; inputs, season, recipes, targets, reserves, and situated modifiers apply afterward and may bound the result. Cultivation and Supply Preparation each retain their own fractional remainder; Caretaker Provisioning retains one shared remainder rather than duplicating it across its two outputs. A 1.5 example releases **1 → 2 → 1** across successive eligible DAWNs. Provisioning uses **Prepare**, **Hold**, and explanatory **Unavailable** states; targets limit production and protected reserves prevent unrelated use. Tools branch to Crafter Projects and never appear as passive output. A compact inset distinguishes pooled Stores from their visible Storehouse manifestation and shows that selecting any Storehouse opens the same Resources screen |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable diagram, tables, and typography; no historical economy dashboard controls its form |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 3.3, 3.4, 4.6, and 5.1; Appendices D and E |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | Both sides of the Practice-and-Citizen gate are mandatory; addition replaces pairing, multiplication, and the retired minus-one rule; the Well-Placed bonus remains one-half; other Practices invent no output; transformation never creates missing inputs; reserves remain visible and releasable before commitment; Supplies remain distinct from Tools; and no provisional quantity is presented as tuned fact |

##### `AUG-3.6-02` — DAWN: The Day Becomes State

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 3.6, Beautification through Resources and Projects |
| **Reader task** | Follow the exact boundary at which elapsed daily contribution, consumption, production, Projects, recovery, and civic state become the new day's facts |
| **Augment form** | One-page editable ordered-accounting diagram with a subordinate Beautification path |
| **Working subject** | **DAWN — The Day Becomes State** |
| **Scale** | One full page within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | None; temporal order and dependency are the subject |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. A particular DAWN would obscure which operations are universal and which results depend upon one campaign state |
| **Register** | World clock and DWELL accounting boundary |
| **Subjects** | Sustenance consumption; transformation; spoilage; passive production; Beautification and Frills; Project progress; recovery and availability; Place and Civic Balance recalculation; meaningful report |
| **Incoming state** | A playable day has elapsed with its Citizens, Practices, Projects, Stores, and conditions in their final pre-DAWN state |
| **Outgoing state** | Whole results have been released, new availability has become true, fractions remain carried, civic state is recalculated, and the new playable day begins |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 3.6 DAWN accounting and Beautification; Appendix D DAWN reference order and Frill rules |
| **Semantic content** | Ordered sequence: consume **one Sustenance per total resident Body Unit**, including Citizens Away and normally Perishable before Durable, plus the explicitly tuned addition for Citizens in the Dependency span; resolve active preservation and Supply preparation; apply aggregate Perishable spoilage; add passive production and release whole chunks; add Beautification and create any earned Frill; add Project progress and complete eligible Projects; advance Recovery Days and Young development spans; recalculate usable Places, Spatial Alignment, Housing and Role Balance, and Colony Pressure; report meaningful changes. Body Units are totaled before demand is calculated, so two Mice create the same ordinary daily demand as one Rabbit or Squirrel. Fractions remain behind the scenes. At the recovery step, each Home-present Wounded Citizen occupying a usable Hearth records exactly one Recovery Day; no fractional Recovery Day or named Caretaker contribution exists, and completion releases the Patient Load and Hearth. Each Young Citizen advances through Dependency and Learning without staffing-based acceleration; the current span determines Caretaker or Leader Load, and completion opens Maturity and the Citizen's Civic Share. A Residence or Practice completed now is usable in the new day and first contributes passive output at the following DAWN. The subordinate Beautification path shows ordinary Builder, Watchkeeper, and Leader Civic Shares contributing while Project-bound Citizens and Well-Placed Practices add nothing; an unspecified threshold creates one small authored, persistent, mechanically inert Frill during Quiet Equilibrium, which may invite EMBODY |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable sequence and typography; no clock dial, economy meter, or production UI mockup is required |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 2.5, 3.2, 3.4, 3.7, and 3.8; Appendix D |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | The accounting order is complete and unambiguous; the one-Sustenance-per-resident-Body-Unit baseline is visible and includes Citizens Away; spoilage precedes new passive production; whole awards occur only at DAWN while ordinary production fractions persist; Wound recovery advances by one discrete eligible Recovery Day rather than fractional Share contribution; completion affects the new day without retroactive production; Beautification remains outside material production and optimization; Frills provide no bonus; and exact thresholds, spoilage, situated modifiers, and wider economy balance remain reserved for tuning |

The production spread explains what may flow. The DAWN page explains when that
flow becomes state. Together they preserve a small economy without requiring a
general-purpose resource simulation or an in-fiction accounting scene.

#### 3.7 Population and Settlement Growth

##### `AUG-3.7-01` — One Population, Four Views

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 3.7, Population at Home through Supporting a larger Colony |
| **Reader task** | Distinguish belonging, physical presence, present civic availability, and dependency while understanding how Body Units relate Citizens to Housing without reducing anyone to labor or capacity |
| **Augment form** | One-page editable population-and-housing diagram integrated with designed prose |
| **Working subject** | **One Population, Four Views** |
| **Scale** | One full page within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | None; this establishes the stable population grammar used by the following three histories |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. Named Citizens enter the next spread after the four views and Housing relationships are unambiguous |
| **Register** | DWELL reference |
| **Subjects** | Colony Roster; Citizens at Home; Available Civic Population; Dependents and Patients; Body Units; Core Residence; Guest Residence Fit; Housing Pressure |
| **Incoming state** | Chapter 3.6 has established the material obligations and civic contributions that a changing population affects |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can place any Citizen in the correct population views, calculate ordinary Core Housing relationships, and understand why absence, incapacity, dependency, or shortfall never removes citizenship |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 3.7 population, capability, obligation, and settlement-support rules; Chapter 3.3 Residence Capacity; current Body-Unit relationships |
| **Semantic content** | Four related but non-interchangeable views: the Colony Roster includes every living named Citizen who belongs, including young Citizens, Guests, Patients, and those Away; Citizens at Home filters physical presence; Available Civic Population filters Home-present adults able to sustain Roles or Projects; Dependents and Patients identifies present lives requiring support. One Rabbit or Squirrel is one Body Unit; two individual Mice are one Body Unit while remaining two complete Citizens. An ordinary Core Residence supports two Body Units and a Well-Placed one supports three: two or three Rabbits or Squirrels, or four or six Mice. Current Guests follow their individual Residence Fit through dedicated accommodation unless an explicit Practice-sharing exception applies. Absence or incapacity removes a current Civic Share rather than citizenship. Housing shortfall creates Pressure and provisional accommodation, never eviction. Supporting growth depends upon Housing, Sustenance, routes, care, Readiness, and preparation rather than one population cap |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable diagram and typography; no population counter, resident grid, or housing meter controls its form |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 3.2, 3.3, 4.1–4.3, and 5.1; Appendices D and E |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | Roster and labor remain distinct; every Mouse remains individually named; Body Units normalize scale rather than personhood; Core Housing values remain 2/3 Body Units and 4/6 Mice; Guest Residence Fit does not become generic Core capacity; shortfall never evicts; and no abstract population ceiling appears |

##### `AUG-3.7-02` — Three Ways Home Grows

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 3.7, Three paths of growth through Scale and continuity |
| **Reader task** | Understand Wanderer integration, Nesting, and Guest residency as distinct relationships and commitments that add particular lives, histories, capabilities, and obligations |
| **Augment form** | Two-page species-balanced narrative triptych with three coordinated worked histories |
| **Working subject** | **Three Ways Home Grows** |
| **Scale** | One two-page spread within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | Equal Mouse, Rabbit, and Squirrel continuity through three different forms of arrival and belonging |
| **Narrative treatment** | Three compact recurring histories. Tassel joins Morningside as a Wanderer; Tuck and Seam enter Rushbottom through its first Nesting history; WHOOT joins New Elderbank after a high listening hollow fulfills Guest Residence Fit. The examples belong to their Story Colonies without assigning any path exclusively to one Species |
| **Register** | Home MEET and DWELL aftermath, with prior contact permitted to have begun Away |
| **Subjects** | Tassel and Morningside; Tuck, Seam, and Rushbottom; WHOOT and New Elderbank; selected established Citizens necessary to make welcome and care visible without defining every relationship involved |
| **Incoming state** | Tassel is an adult Squirrel Wanderer outside Morningside's Roster; Rushbottom has no young from its first Nesting history; WHOOT is known but New Elderbank has not yet fulfilled the agreed Residence Fit |
| **Outgoing state** | Tassel is an equal Core Citizen with a Prior-life Tale and an available adult Civic Share; Tuck and Seam are two fully named young Mice adding one Body Unit of needs and no Civic Share until Maturity; WHOOT is one named Owl Guest Citizen whose high listening hollow, continuing support, and ordinary obligations make residency credible |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 3.7 Wanderer, Nesting, and Guest-residency rules; current Story-Colony identity and Tier histories; canon images for every identifiable Citizen; current Owl Residence Fit and WHOOT identity |
| **Semantic content** | Each field uses the same three-part grammar: opening relationship, Colony commitment, and resulting named life with new obligations. Morningside may offer Tassel permanent welcome, temporary refuge, help onward, or refusal according to actual capacity; the selected Story history resolves in welcome. Rushbottom's rare green-season Nesting commitment protects future accommodation, Sustenance, Hearth, Caretaker support, Leader support, and protection. Tuck and Seam remain separate Citizens sharing one Body Unit: their Young life passes first through a Dependency span with enlarged Sustenance, Caretaker Load, and intensive Hearth commitment, then through a Learning span with ordinary Sustenance and Leader Load before Maturity begins their Civic Shares. Exact Day counts and added Sustenance remain tuning values; neither staffing nor player action accelerates them. New Elderbank's relationship with WHOOT becomes residency only when the high listening hollow and other agreed Terms of Hospitality are true; the Owl then joins the Roster and supplies an ordinary Civic Share while available. A shared note states that every Core Colony may grow through all three paths and that all growth remains optional |
| **Asset lineage** | New current-spec narrative compositions derived mandatorily from the canon Colony and Citizen images. Manifest row 72 may inform the equal triptych rhythm only; its old subjects, environments, and details do not survive |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.5, 4.1–4.5, and 6.2–6.3; Appendix H Story-Colony histories |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved; exact supporting cast, moment within each arrival, and final page composition remain for the asset brief |
| **Acceptance test** | All three Story Colonies receive equal substantive weight; every arrival remains a particular Citizen rather than a population token; the three growth paths stay mechanically distinct and universally available; the Mouse young remain two individuals and one Body Unit; no parents are assigned by the player or invented by the augment; WHOOT's accommodation is specific rather than generic Guest housing; and each result carries visible obligation as well as capability |

The first page prevents population from collapsing into workforce. The triptych
then shows three different ways that belonging changes the Roster, Home, and
future responsibilities while keeping every arrival particular.

#### 3.8 EMBODY

##### `AUG-3.8-01` — Permission for Attention

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 3.8, Access and invitation through Experience families |
| **Reader task** | Understand when EMBODY becomes available, how the player enters it, what Participation and Presence mean, and why an experience is an invitation rather than another obligation |
| **Augment form** | One-page editable access-and-experience diagram integrated with designed prose |
| **Working subject** | **Permission for Attention** |
| **Scale** | One full page within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | None; the following Gale sequence supplies the particular experience |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. The diagram protects the complete access grammar before one Species and experience family embody it |
| **Register** | EMBODY entered from DWELL and returning to DWELL |
| **Subjects** | Quiet Equilibrium; present and available Citizen; safe usable Place; invitation; Participation; Presence; the seven experience families; Open Table as exceptional collective Presence; natural release; safe closure |
| **Incoming state** | DWELL has established a credible sanctuary and Quiet Equilibrium is present |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can identify an eligible invitation, distinguish the two primary forms, and understand how ordinary departure or renewed urgency returns attention to DWELL or MEET |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 3.8 Access and invitation, Participation and Presence, Experience families, Memory and reward, and Boundaries; Chapter 7.1 EMBODY presentation |
| **Semantic content** | The ordinary path reads **Quiet Equilibrium + present available Citizen + safe usable Place → invitation → Participation and/or Presence → natural release → DWELL**. Flow Traversal, Small Work, Sensory Repose, Social Play, Comfort and Care, Observation and Watch, and Weather Enjoyment form a non-progressive family field. Open Table is not another family: it is rare large-cast Presence entered through an authored gathering rather than a Citizen and may contain several families simultaneously. Its path reads **Home + Quiet Equilibrium + safe usable Gathering Place with Open Table improvement + present eligible gathering → invitation → collective Presence → natural release → DWELL**. No particular Citizen or active Leader is required after the improvement exists. Ordinary entry closes the DWELL view toward the Citizen and settles ordinarily into close third person. Open Table instead settles into guided, animal-scale collective observation without giving the player a Citizen body. Controls and attention remain specific to the moment. The player may leave without penalty. Loss of Quiet Equilibrium closes the experience safely before an urgent situation opens MEET. Tharn, unavailability, an unsafe Place, or Away calm never opens EMBODY. The experience itself is the reward; there is no output multiplier, Civic Share, currency, score, failure loop, or automatic memory record |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable diagram and typography; no minigame selector or activity menu controls its form |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.4, 3.1, 3.2, and 7.1; Appendix I |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | Quiet Equilibrium remains permission rather than currency; ordinary access stays Home-only and Citizen-specific; Open Table is the explicit gathering-entry exception and requires its defined Gathering Place improvement and an authored gathering; Participation and Presence may flow into one another; the seven families do not become tracks; collective Presence never grants control of a Citizen; closure under urgency remains safe; and EMBODY offers neither required chores nor mechanical superiority |

|---|---|

| **Register** | EMBODY |

The activity slows naturally into Presence at a high familiar junction: moving weather and traffic may be watched and heard as ordinary Home experience rather than Telegraph work. Controls narrow with the moment; Gale retains pace, posture, hesitation, and attention. Nearby Citizens remain named inhabitants rather than props. Looking, listening, settling, and waiting remain valid participation. Departure returns to DWELL without penalty |
| **Asset lineage** | New current-spec presentation derived mandatorily from Gale's canon image and the selected Tier II Morningside visual state established for `AUG-3.2-01`; no separate generic Squirrel backplate may replace that continuity |

| **Priority** | Essential |

| **Acceptance test** | Gale remains the same recognizable Citizen seen in DWELL; fishing line appears only after its established acquisition; CONNECT is felt through body and Place rather than explained as a bonus; the traversal remains pleasurable and safe; observation does not become Watchkeeper labor; and no output, stat, score, or automatic historical importance is awarded |

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|

Every visible participant remains a named Citizen rather than a whole-Colony unit. The access diagram defines EMBODY, Gale demonstrates its ordinary individual
form, and Open Table shows Presence at exceptional collective breadth. Their
sequence lets Part III end by inhabiting the sanctuary whose civic and material
logic the Part has established.

#### Part III horizontal audit

**Outcome: pass.** The first mapping pass is coherent and requires no Chapter
remap before Part IV. Production must preserve the distinctions and restraint
below.

| Audit dimension | Finding | Production constraint |
|---|---|---|
| **Core-Species presence** | Mouse carries the Part threshold and Pressure example; Squirrel carries the literal DWELL and individual EMBODY presentations; Rabbit participates in the placement comparison, Guest-residency history, and Open Table close. The three-path growth spread gives all three equal simultaneous weight. | Do not expand incidental Mouse or Squirrel appearances merely because their current-development images are plentiful. Rabbit's lighter major-image presence in this Part is intentional after the sustained Rabbit opening in Part 0 and Part I. |
| **Story continuity** | Rushbottom moves from ordinary Morning to threatened recognizable ground; Morningside moves from the fixed DWELL view into intimate time with the same Gale; New Elderbank continues through WHOOT's residency and a later Open Table gathering. | Asset briefs must carry exact Tier, roster, Place state, possessions, injuries, Guest arrivals, and prior appearances forward. No scene may silently reset a Colony to a convenient generic state. |
| **Register coverage** | DWELL receives one literal fixed view and the civic systems acting beneath it; Home MEET receives one worked Pressure sequence; EMBODY receives access grammar, individual experience, and collective exception. TRAVEL and RISK properly remain outside the Part. | Reuse the shared interface language established in Part I without turning every system diagram into a literal screen. |
| **Narrative rhythm** | Ordinary life opens the Part; designed definitions and systems establish civic credibility; Water at Rushbottom supplies consequence; quiet economy and population explanation follow; three growth histories restore particular lives; Gale and Open Table end in companionship. | Preserve the quiet interval across Chapters 3.6 and the first half of 3.7. Do not add a narrative incident to every diagram or Role entry. |
| **Augment-form rhythm** | One threshold plate is followed by designed prose, a fixed literal view, comparative and causal infographics, typography-led Role entries, one worked Home MEET, narrative triptych, one intimate game-presentation sequence, and a partial-page closing scene. | No interior full-page artwork is added. Full pages inside Chapters remain diagrams, infographics, designed prose, or literal game presentations. |
| **System overlap** | Civic Balance, Places, Roles, Projects, Pressure, production, DAWN, population, and EMBODY recur where later Chapters need their consequences, but each has one governing explanatory home. | Chapter 3.4 owns Project Queue operation; the DAWN page shows only its accounting position. Chapter 3.3 owns Spatial Alignment; Chapter 3.6 applies its half-yield. Chapter 3.2 owns Civic Balance; Chapter 3.5 shows how the world tests it. Cross-reference rather than restate. |

The remaining Part III decisions belong to asset briefs rather than structure:
the Rushbottom threshold cast and activity; the selected response and aftermath
for Water at Rushbottom; supporting Citizens in the three growth histories;
Gale's exact way, weather, and release; and the Tier, roster, and occasion for
Open Table at New Elderbank.

| Field | Approved mapping |

| **Incoming state** | Part III has ended with civic sanctuary inhabited collectively and through EMBODY |
