The threshold intentionally broadens recognition rather than returning to the
book's most familiar anchors. Its three subjects should be chosen from Citizens
whose established identities warrant attention but who have not yet carried a
major scene. Exact selection belongs to the asset brief so the completed
book-wide recurrence ledger can identify the genuinely underused lives.

#### 4.1 Citizenhood

##### `AUG-4.1-01` — The Player Across Many Lives

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 4.1, Citizenhood and fractional player characters |
| **Reader task** | Understand how player capability, attention, vulnerability, possessions, relationships, and continuity are distributed across a Roster of complete individual lives |
| **Augment form** | One-page editable typography-led topology integrated with the Chapter prose |
| **Working subject** | **The Player Across Many Lives** |
| **Scale** | One self-contained landscape page immediately after the Part threshold |
| **Narrative function** | None; the threshold triptych has already supplied three particular lives |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. Repeating portraiture immediately would weaken the threshold and risk turning one additional Citizen into the universal explanatory mascot |
| **Register** | Cross-Register player-and-Roster reference |
| **Subjects** | The player; the Colony Roster; fractional player characters; distributed action, exposure, possession, relationship, and attention; Body Units and Civic Shares |
| **Incoming state** | The Part threshold has presented three equally weighted, less-used Citizens as recognizable lives without interface classification |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can explain fractional player character as distributed player continuity rather than partial personhood and can distinguish individual identity from Body-Unit-scaled civic action |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 4.1 Citizenhood and One civic architecture; current Roster, Body-Unit, Civic Share, Focus, and continuity rules |
| **Semantic content** | One player relationship opening into many named Citizens rather than one avatar or autonomous workforce. Capability, exposure, possessions, relationships, and possible attention distribute across the Roster. Each Citizen carries appearance, voice, temperament, habits, relationships, possessions, routines, accumulated history, and a possible future without those becoming ratings. One Rabbit, Squirrel, or current Guest is one Body Unit; two individual Mice are one Body Unit while remaining two complete Citizens. Body Units scale civic and Away contribution while names, choices, vulnerability, Focus, and consequence remain particular |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable typography and connective diagram only. The threshold portraits are not repeated or cropped into cards |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.2, 1.4, 3.7, 4.2–4.6, and 6.1; Appendices E and H; `AUG-4.1-02` |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | The page reads completely by itself in a single-page PDF view; fractional player character describes distributed player continuity rather than partial personhood; Mouse halves never pair identities; no aptitude band, productivity rank, personality approval, autonomous refusal, rarity, or generic Citizen card appears; and the topology makes many possible centers of action feel coherent rather than fragmented |

##### `AUG-4.1-02` — A Citizen Persists

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 4.1, persistent state and conditional contribution |
| **Reader task** | Distinguish enduring citizenship from changing location, availability, civic contribution, bodily state, possessions, relationships, and historical continuity |
| **Augment form** | One-page editable state-continuity diagram integrated with the Chapter prose |
| **Working subject** | **A Citizen Persists** |
| **Scale** | One self-contained landscape page consecutive with, but compositionally independent from, `AUG-4.1-01` |
| **Narrative function** | None; the page explains the shared state architecture that later narrative depends upon |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. Particular Citizens appear throughout the rest of Part IV, while this page establishes why their changes persist consistently |
| **Register** | Cross-Register Citizen-state reference |
| **Subjects** | Belonging; location; availability; Civic contribution; identity; body and Item state; relationships; Campaign Memory; state-changing events |
| **Incoming state** | `AUG-4.1-01` has established the Roster as many complete lives through whom one campaign is played |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader trusts that one Citizen has one current truth read consistently by every Register and reference view |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 4.1 persistent-state rules; current Launch, Homecoming, Project, injury, treatment, Maturity, death, Roster, Almanac, Tale, and Record rules |
| **Semantic content** | Belonging remains in the living Roster; location is one current Home or Away position; age, injury, treatment, absence, or another real condition determines availability; an available adult supplies the Civic Share of their Body Unit; names, relationships, Items, Wound, Maiming, memory, and death follow the individual. Launch, Homecoming, Project commitment, injury, treatment, Maturity, and death each update the relevant fact once. DWELL, EMBODY, TRAVEL, RISK, MEET, Roster, Almanac, Tale, and Record read that same fact according to their function. Conditional contribution may cease while citizenship and historical belonging remain true |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable typography and state diagram only; no repeated threshold portrait, paper-doll inventory, status card, or duplicated database view |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.2, 1.4, 3.7, 4.4–4.6, 5.1, 5.8, and 6.1; Appendices E and H; `AUG-4.1-01` |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | The page reads completely by itself in a single-page PDF view; absence, dependency, incapacity, Project commitment, or Away location never cancels belonging; state changes occur once rather than separately in each Register; civic contribution remains conditional; death ends living membership without deleting historical belonging; and the diagram does not become a universal Citizen card or dashboard |

No additional portrait accompanies Chapter 4.1. The threshold establishes
particularity through faces and places; these two autonomous pages separately
establish distributed player continuity and durable Citizen state.

#### 4.2 The Three Core Species

##### `AUG-4.2-01` — Three Complete Civilizations

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 4.2, the Mouse, Rabbit, and Squirrel entries |
| **Reader task** | Encounter each Core Species as a complete playable civilization whose body changes Home, Away, Crossing, risk, and campaign choice without changing civic worth |
| **Augment form** | Three matched two-page Species sequences, each formed from two independently titled and complete designed pages with editable comparison structure and one half-page character-example illustration across the sequence |
| **Working subject** | **Three Complete Civilizations — Mouse · Rabbit · Squirrel** |
| **Scale** | Two autonomous landscape pages per Species; six pages total before the concluding comparison |
| **Narrative function** | Core-Species equality, deeper recognition of the three less-used threshold Citizens, and bodily particularity without a new event |
| **Narrative treatment** | Three recurring lived portraits. Each threshold Citizen appears in an ordinary bodily relationship with their own Home or immediate environment, giving the profile a particular life without making that Citizen the specimen or universal personality of the Species |
| **Register** | Cross-Register species profile; Home, TRAVEL, RISK, and MEET consequences are related without simulating one literal session |
| **Subjects** | The selected less-used Mouse from Rushbottom, Rabbit from New Elderbank, and Squirrel from Morningside; their bodies and current identity details; the complete Core-Species rules each profile explains |
| **Incoming state** | The threshold has introduced the three Citizens intimately and Chapter 4.1 has established their shared civic and historical architecture |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader understands each Species in its own terms and can identify the characteristic advantage, meaningful decision, Home expression, and Crossing grammar arising from its body |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 4.2 Mouse, Rabbit, Squirrel, and comparison prose; current Story-Colony and Citizen identities; canon Citizen and Colony imagery; Chapters 3.3, 5.2, and 5.3 as mechanical owners of placement, TRAVEL, and RISK detail |
| **Semantic content** | Every Species receives two pages with its name and sufficient premise repeated for independent reading. Provisional title grammar is **Mouse — Body and Home** / **Mouse — Away and Crossing**, then the same matched pair for Rabbit and Squirrel. The first page begins from the animal: overall introduction and bodily tempo; body and civic scale; Home operator and civilizational shape. The second begins from expedition consequence: characteristic Away advantage; the decision that advantage creates; Crossing expression; campaign character. Mouse: quick heartbeat and speed of thought, 0.5 Body Unit and Civic Share, six secured Carry per Citizen, JOIN, two personal Item sets per Body Unit, up to six named Mice in a full party, and a three-to-six-waypoint scurry. Rabbit: vigilant stillness and explosive movement, one Body Unit and Share, GATHER, Body-Unit travel contributions reaching double progress with three Rabbit Body Units, and one decisive line through a temporal opening. Squirrel: grasp, balance, reach, and continuous judgment, one Body Unit and Share, CONNECT, ten optional Strained Carry per Body Unit with visible Jostle risk, and a flowing trajectory with limited redirects. Each sequence retains the illustrative Manor House, Cul-de-Sac, or Web image while Residence and Practice names remain shared |
| **Asset lineage** | Three new matched half-page illustrations derived mandatorily from the selected threshold Citizens' canon images and their Colony states. Historical Species portraits may inform scale or medium only. Each image shows an ordinary embodied moment rather than a labeled character sheet, heroic class portrait, or isolated wildlife study |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 2.1, 3.3, 4.1, 5.2, and 5.3; Appendices G and H |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved direction; exact threshold Citizens, illustrated actions or repose, crop, setting, and profile order remain to be briefed together |
| **Acceptance test** | All three Species receive equal pages, illustration area, textual specificity, vulnerability, and dignity; each of the six pages reads completely by itself in a single-page PDF view; every first page begins from the animal body before mechanics; every second page restates enough bodily premise to explain Away consequences; the featured Citizen remains an individual rather than a representative temperament; Home and Away differences arise from bodies rather than cosmetic culture; no Species gains a general aptitude or extra action; and no illustration becomes an interior full-page plate |

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Closing of Chapter 4.2 |

| **Augment form** | One-page editable comparative matrix with a compact mixed-party note |
| **Working subject** | **Species Expression at a Glance** |
| **Scale** | One full page following the three profiles |

The mixed-party note states that only bodies actually present supply traits: Mouse contributes its individual secured Carry and Item positions; Rabbit Body Units contribute Rabbit TRAVEL progress; Squirrel Body Units contribute optional Strained Carry; the party follows the selected Core Species RISK grammar except for the approved Crow and Gull Flyer expression addressed later |

The matched blocks let each Species be encountered whole. The matrix then
supports direct comparison without fragmenting the preceding profiles into a
repeating sequence of isolated mechanical tests.

#### 4.3 Guest Citizens

##### `AUG-4.3-01` — Hospitality Is a Relationship

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 4.3, Hospitality and Guest life |
| **Reader task** | Understand how a named non-Core life becomes known, welcomed, accommodated, and joined to the Roster without becoming a recruitment roll or collectible unlock |
| **Augment form** | One-page editable hospitality-and-residence flow diagram |
| **Working subject** | **Hospitality Is a Relationship** |
| **Scale** | One full page within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | None; the Chapter's Story Guests and the later presence triptych provide particular lives |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. A general flow must preserve acceptance, delay, refusal, and unrealized welcome as possible outcomes rather than imply one canonical successful recruitment scene |
| **Register** | MEET leading into DWELL, Builder Project, Arrival, and continuing Home life |
| **Subjects** | Recognition; Terms of Hospitality; Residence Fit; Builder Project; Arrival; Roster; dedicated Guest Residence; explicit Practice-sharing exceptions; collective-bodied Citizens; later loss of fit |
| **Incoming state** | A named non-Core animal has become known through contact, shared interest, promise, obligation, or unresolved relationship but does not yet belong to the Colony |
| **Outgoing state** | When the agreed Terms are true, one named Guest Citizen joins Home and the Roster with full civic and historical standing; other choices remain valid campaign history |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 4.3 Hospitality and Guest life; current Residence Fits, Practice-sharing exceptions, collective-bodied households, and Builder Project rules |
| **Semantic content** | **Recognition → Terms of Hospitality → Residence Project → Terms fulfilled → Arrival and Roster**. Recognition begins through MEET with a named life. Terms identify species Residence Fit and any particular relational or safety promise. A Builder establishes or adapts the Residence. Acceptance, delay, refusal, and unrealized welcome remain meaningful. The ordinary rule is one dedicated Place housing one Guest Citizen, with fit judged as a whole rather than by generic capacity. Five explicit Signature exceptions incorporate one Residence into one named Practice: Turtle with Garden, Bumblebees with Garden, Bat with Workshop, Pigeon with Gathering Place, and Possum with Hearth. These create no general mixed-use rule. Fireflies and Bumblebees each form one collective-bodied Citizen, Body Unit, Share, Residence, Role, Signature, and subject of consequence. Later loss of fit creates visible accommodation Pressure and may suspend a dependent Signature; it never evicts the Citizen |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable flow and residence diagrams; no recruitment screen, species-collection track, or Guest card system controls its form |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 1.5, 3.3, 3.7, and 6.1; Appendix H |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved |
| **Acceptance test** | Relationship precedes residency; Residence Fit and relational Terms remain distinct; the five incorporations remain explicit exceptions; collective bodies remain one continuous Citizen without individually administered members; loss of fit never cancels citizenship; and no Guest is automatic, required, ranked, or acquired through a roll |

##### `AUG-4.3-02` — A Wider Menagerie

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 4.3, Guests at a glance and all Expedition and Resident Guest profiles |

| **Working subject** | **A Wider Menagerie** |
| **Scale** | Exact pagination follows type measure; all twenty profiles receive equal structural completeness without twenty half-page portraits |
| **Narrative function** | None; this is the complete reader-facing Guest roster and profile reference |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none within the profile sequence. Particular Story Guests appear before and after it so the reference does not turn species into interchangeable named characters |
| **Register** | Cross-Register Guest reference |
| **Subjects** | Eight Expedition Guest species and twelve Resident Guest species, with current short names, longer natural names, Carry or Home Role, Signature, Residence Fit, body or ecology, and limits |
| **Incoming state** | `AUG-4.3-01` has established the common relationship and accommodation path shared by every Guest |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can identify all twenty current species, distinguish Expedition and Resident types, and use each Signature without expecting it to define the whole Citizen or expand beyond its authored situation |

Resident table second: Owl, Sparrow, Toad, Fireflies, Groundhog, Turtle, Bumblebees, Mole, Bat, Pigeon, Skunk, and Possum with fixed Home Role, Home Signature, and Residence Fit. Tables and labels use only canonical short names. Profile prose may use Raccoon, Crow, Gull, Red Fox, Weasel, Hedgehog, Garter Snake, American Mink, Barred Owl, Song Sparrow, American Toad, Firefly Family, Groundhog, Painted Turtle, Bumblebee Household, Eastern Mole, Little Brown Bat, Rock Pigeon, Striped Skunk, and Possum, Opossum, or Virginia Opossum as established. Every profile follows one editorial sequence: body or ecology; Carry or fixed Home Role; exact Signature; Residence Fit; bounded limitation. Expedition Guests may Launch and receive an individually authored Home Role; Resident Guests remain Home and sustain the listed Role. All current Guests are one Body Unit and one Civic Share while available. Recruitment remains optional at every Tier and no system requires a complete Guest roster |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable tables and typography with a restrained, consistent silhouette or ecological-detail class. No profile receives a collectible-card frame, rarity treatment, full-page portrait, or more visual weight because its Signature appears more dramatic |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 4.1, 4.6, 5.1–5.5, and 7.1; Appendices E, G, and H |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved; exact page count, silhouette medium, and profile grouping remain for pagination and the asset brief |
| **Acceptance test** | All twenty species are equally findable and complete; short names govern labels; longer names create prose variation without multiplying rules; Snake remains the six-Carry outlier; Expedition Carry otherwise stays within eight to twelve; Signatures remain one bounded capability; Resident Roles remain fixed; current Guests remain one Body Unit; and the sequence reads as civic reference rather than a bestiary or collection checklist |

|---|---|

Core Citizens respond as familiar neighbors rather than an audience for animal spectacle. Sound, light, movement, routine, and changed expectation carry the meaning |

The hospitality diagram explains how belonging becomes possible, the profile
sequence supplies complete reference, and the final triptych demonstrates why
Guest life matters after its mechanical exception has become familiar.

#### 4.4 Relationships and Family

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|

| **Canon inputs** | Current Rowan, Cobb, and Morningside canon images; current Morningside Tier history; Chapter 4.4 and Appendix H relationship rules |
| **Semantic content** | Five event markers with short editable account, relevant Register, and the relationship expression made visible in that situation. A final present-state field may name several simultaneous qualitative truths—Founders, companions in survival, persistent civic disagreement, and mutual accountability—without arranging them on a positive or negative axis. A compact Register key explains that relationship effects belong to the particular situation or owning system |
| **Asset lineage** | New diagram and typography with small current-spec image fragments derived mandatorily from canon Citizen and Colony imagery. No affection bar, heart track, bond level, social-rank badge, or generic relationship currency |

| **Priority** | Essential |

|---|---|

It also states the positive division of responsibility: Residence accommodates bodies; Hearth provides care; Family belongs to the Citizens who recognize and live it. Family may overlap across species, generations, origins, Residences, and Core or Guest status |

The first page follows one relationship through time. The second widens from
that history into distinct forms of family. Their sequence is useful, but each
page carries its own heading, premise, evidence, and conclusion so laptop
reading never depends upon facing-page composition.

#### 4.5 The Life Course

##### `AUG-4.5-01` — One Life, Many Futures

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 4.5, The Life Course |
| **Reader task** | Understand the complete v0.5 life-course architecture without mistaking it for a universal aging simulation, rank ladder, or replacement cycle |
| **Augment form** | One-page editable life-architecture infographic organized into Origins, Life in the Colony, and Continuity |
| **Working subject** | **One Life, Many Futures** |
| **Scale** | One self-contained landscape page |
| **Narrative function** | Particularity through named examples without creating a new canonical Maturity scene |
| **Narrative treatment** | Brief established examples ground different entrances into Colony life: Founders with shared history; Daisy or Wick as campaign-born; Tuck and Seam as two named Mouse littermates sharing one young Body Unit; one established Wanderer; and one established Guest. The examples remain fragments rather than one invented cradle-to-death biography |
| **Register** | Cross-Register Citizen reference and Campaign Memory |
| **Subjects** | Selected established Story Citizens representing Founding, Nesting, Wanderer, and Guest origins; exact Wanderer, Guest, and choice between Daisy or Wick remain for the asset brief |
| **Incoming state** | A named life enters or already belongs to the Colony with an origin, relationships, Residence association, and a growing Tale |
| **Outgoing state** | The Citizen's future remains open through Young or Adult life; death, when it occurs through an authored consequence, ends present contribution while historical belonging persists |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapters 4.1, 4.4, 4.5, and 6.1; current Story-Colony Citizen identities and canon images; Appendix H life-course reference |
| **Semantic content** | **Origins:** Founding, Nesting, adoption or other authored young arrival, Wanderer, and Guest converge on equal Citizen standing while preserving different prior histories. **Life in the Colony:** Young and Adult are the only mechanical stages. Young moves through sequential Dependency and Learning support spans: enlarged Sustenance, Caretaker Load, and Hearth commitment give way to ordinary Sustenance and Leader Load. Maturity is the sole visible transition, ending Young status and development Load while opening ordinary Civic Share and Role eligibility; expedition eligibility remains separate. Adult life opens into many valid forms—Home work, Away participation, care, leadership, disability, changed availability, veteran life, or quiet ordinary life—without arranging them as progression. **Continuity:** death removes present action and civic contribution while the Record, closed Tale, relationships, Keepsakes, possessions, Places, inheritance, grief, and remembrance preserve the particular life in Campaign Memory |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable infographic and restrained current-spec Citizen fragments derived mandatorily from canon imagery where named examples appear. The central architecture remains typographic and diagrammatic rather than a generated illustration |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 3.7, 4.1, 4.4, and 6.1; Appendix H |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved direction; exact named examples, visual grammar, and the relative weight of Continuity remain for the asset brief |
| **Acceptance test** | The page reads completely by itself in a single-page PDF view; every origin reaches equal civic standing; Tuck and Seam remain two Citizens; Young and Adult remain the only mechanical stages; Maturity appears once without an experience track; adulthood remains open rather than hierarchical; death is rare and authored rather than timed; and continuity does not frame any later arrival as replacement |

The page holds the whole architecture at Core depth. Appendix H retains the
exact Load, Civic Share, origin, eligibility, aging, and continuity rules, so
the body needs no additional age table or invented biographical demonstration.

| **Incoming state** | The MEET has been framed and one response has been provisionally selected; relevant expressions are visible, the choice remains reversible, and Focus has not resolved |
| **Outgoing state** | The worked continuation commits one personal-Item expression, identifies its bearer as Focus, resolves the collective outcome first, and returns consumption, hazard, damage, or continuing Item state to its actual owner |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapters 1.5 and 4.6; Appendix E Item, expression, Commit, Focus, and aftermath rules; the exact `AUG-1.5-01` asset and selected Rushbottom canon Citizen imagery |
| **Semantic content** | The current five contested response families—**CONTEST → EVADE → PARLEY → YIELD → WITHDRAW**—remain visible in fixed order, with inactive families greyed and explained. One provisional response exposes its applicable expressions. External Citizen boxes compare **Tool** as named, persistent, reusable capability; **Supply** as an anonymous body-scaled intervention assigned for one expedition and consumed on commitment; and **Keepsake** as a personally persistent emotional object relevant only in a narrow circumstance. Supply reference includes Binding, Device, Offering, and Remedy, with the MEET stating the exact effect. Arrows identify response selection, expression relevance, one chosen personal Item or none, Commit, bearer, Focus, shared result, personal consequence, and final Item state. A concise note allows one applicable bodily Guest Signature to coexist without becoming an Item or generic stacked bonus |
| **Asset lineage** | Complete v0.5 remake. The supplied v0.4 **Encounter Screen — Layout Guide** and populated **Wrecked Car Scrap** mockup inspire only the composition of a live interface framed by external explanatory callouts. Their generated text, six-choice strip, Turn terminology, portraits, meters, item logic, and depicted participants carry no authority |
| **Cross-reference** | `AUG-1.5-01`, `AUG-1.5-02`, Chapters 5.2 and 5.5, and Appendix E's later annotated-interface reference |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved direction; exact contested situation, provisional response, Items, selected expression, bearer, and possible result remain to be briefed jointly with `AUG-1.5-01` |
| **Acceptance test** | The page reads completely by itself in a single-page PDF view; the central screen depicts one exact pre-Commit state; the inset alone depicts the later state; editorial callouts cannot be mistaken for game UI; response precedes expression; Focus follows Commit and cannot influence the choice; shared outcome precedes personal consequence; Tool, Supply, Remedy, and Keepsake rules remain distinct; Items occupy no Cargo; no obsolete Health, Spirit, Unity, Fatigue, Negotiation, Momentum, success-chance, or fixed-Round-count meter survives; and the composition never reads as an adventurer loadout screen |

This single annotated page replaces the earlier idea of a separate Item
comparison followed by another large MEET page. Its outer boxes perform the
comparison while the populated interface and small later-state inset prove the
sequence in use.

#### Part IV horizontal audit

| Audit dimension | Result | Production constraint |
|---|---|---|
| **Single-page reading** | Pass after remap. The Part threshold, 4.3 presence triptych, 4.4 pages, 4.5 infographic, and 4.6 annotated MEET are autonomous. Chapter 4.1 now separates distributed player continuity from persistent Citizen state. Each Core-Species profile now consists of two independently titled and complete pages. | Consecutive pages may echo one another but must repeat the Species, Citizen, or system premise needed to understand the current page. No label, comparison, subject, or conclusion may require simultaneous facing-page view. |
| **Core-Species balance** | Pass. The threshold and Chapter 4.2 give Mouse, Rabbit, and Squirrel exact parity. Chapter 4.3 gives all three Story Colonies equal Guest-presence fields. Squirrel carries the long relationship history; Rabbit and Mouse carry the family examples; Chapter 4.5 draws from all three; Rushbottom carries the final mechanical demonstration. | Final asset selection must account for the whole Part rather than equalizing every page. The less-used threshold trio remains the primary balancing opportunity. |
| **Citizen particularity** | Pass. Portraiture opens the Part, persistent-state diagrams explain why individuals remain continuous, Species pages return to the same less-used trio, Guest presence shows ordinary familiarity, relationships accumulate through named histories, and the final MEET makes Item consequence personal. | Canon imagery remains mandatory for every named Citizen. Repetition must create familiarity rather than duplicate the same pose, identity fact, or résumé-like tableau. |
| **Narrative rhythm** | Pass. Intimate portraiture gives way to architecture and Species explanation; Guest ordinary life restores sensory presence; Rowan and Cobb supply long history; family and life course widen the horizon; the Part closes through live decision rather than another portrait. | Preserve typographic and diagrammatic quiet in Chapters 4.1 and 4.5. Do not add a freestanding incident to Citizenhood or invent a Maturity scene merely to increase narrative count. |
| **Reference depth** | Pass provisionally. Core explains complete playable identity and the essential Guest Signatures; Appendix H retains exhaustive relationship, Residence, limitation, life-course, and Story-Colony reference. | Later body-compression may shorten repeated profile prose, but no Signature or identity-bearing distinction is removed before verifying its Appendix owner. |
| **Register coverage** | Pass. DWELL, EMBODY, TRAVEL, RISK, MEET, Roster, Almanac, Tale, and Record appear only where Citizen continuity requires them; no Chapter attempts to reteach an entire Register. | Reuse the common Register grammar and cross-references. Chapter 4.6 magnifies the already planned Chapter 1.5 MEET rather than creating a second encounter system. |
| **Transition to Part V** | Pass. `AUG-4.6-01` ends with known Rushbottom Citizens, a provisional Away response, Commit, Focus, shared result, and personal consequence. Part V can therefore widen from a particular MEET into the complete expedition cycle without another mechanical preface. | The Part V threshold should shift scale from named lives to departure and exposure. It should not repeat a MEET interface, Item anatomy, or Citizen portrait triptych. |

The remaining Part IV decisions belong to coordinated asset briefs: the three
less-used threshold Citizens; their six profile-page actions and environments;
the exact Guest-presence compositions; the selected image moments for Rowan
and Cobb, the two family examples, and the life-course fragments; and the one
Rushbottom encounter jointly governing `AUG-1.5-01` and `AUG-4.6-01`.

### 6.6 Part V — Away: Expedition and Return

Part V has one continuous expedition spine. Gale, Alder, and Reed leave
Morningside in Chapter 5.1 and remain the same accumulating party through
TRAVEL, RISK, Node engagement, consequential MEET, Outpost Stopover, Return, and
Homecoming. Their bodies, Items, Cargo, relationships, and resolved changes
persist from page to page. Other Story Citizens may appear as clearly bounded
historical case studies where one journey should not be forced to contain
every possible consequence. Crossing retains its separately approved complete
Rabbit and Mouse interfaces because Core-Species RISK presentation is the one
book-wide exception to the shared-interface rule.

The plate shows the result of a Launch decision without depicting the decision
itself. It therefore does not contain an interface, response buttons, a Node,
an antagonist, a Crossing in progress, predictive hazards, or visible odds.
Its internal title never appears on the image.

relationship between position and reachable ground. A trail of projected
waypoints would imply a route system and therefore does not appear.

| **Semantic content** | Exact cadence: traveled Bands 1–3 at **1×**, Band 4 at **¾×**, Band 5 at **½×**, Band 6 at **¼×**, and Band 7 unavailable until Stopover. A Band counts when it ends after material travel, including when another action advances time before the span is exhausted. MEET and Node work do not count as TRAVEL or reset cadence; Stopover resets cadence. A committed Stopover may begin wherever the party can physically sleep, advances one Band, ends Tharn, and supplies the defined temporary Wound stabilization through a locked static screen rather than MEET; Chapters 5.6 and 5.7 own the deeper bodily, interruption, and Outpost effects. The Node panel distinguishes quiet passage, brief uncontested use, time-occupying work, and consequential MEET. The interruption panel shows one 10% check after an otherwise eligible exhausted span, at most once per Band, suppression for the next two eligible checks after occurrence, exclusion in a Band already occupied by RISK or another consequential MEET, and minor direct TRAVEL change versus consequential MEET. It presents no card, deck, or named player-facing family. The attention sidebar shows Home inspection, continuing Home clock-bound systems, possible temporary Home MEET focus, and return to the party's exact inert position |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable typography and diagrams; no campsite illustration, card deck, random-event wheel, Fatigue meter, Home/Away dual clock, or autonomous-simulation frame |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 2.5, 3.6, 5.3–5.7, and 5.9; Appendix G; `AUG-5.2-01` and `AUG-5.2-02` |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved; final pagination may redistribute prose around the diagram but may not merge this page into the already information-dense reach infographic |
| **Acceptance test** | All six traveled Bands and their exact reach values are legible; Stopover is a committed locked sleep screen rather than MEET and consumes time; pushing creates declining reach without a generic Fatigue state or automatic Wound or Exposure; situated interruptions remain sparse and subordinate to primary Nodes without becoming a named card system; Home can interrupt attention without moving the party; and every element reads on this page without requiring an adjacent spread |

Together the three pages move from experience to abstraction to cadence:

> **The Field under the player's hand → the ground one Day Band can reach →
> the rhythm that governs continued travel.**

#### 5.3 RISK: Crossing

RISK is the principal exception to the book's usual shared-interface grammar.
The three Core Species do not merely receive different labels or affordances
inside one Crossing screen; each receives one complete, equally weighted
interface mockup whose operative center embodies a different question. The
Chapter's existing comparison table supplies the concise overview. Its
augments demonstrate the difference at full playable scale.

##### `AUG-5.3-04` — Commit, Cross, Resolve

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 5.3, shared Crossing sequence, far-side consequence, Flyer expression, Road Work, and Return Crossing |
| **Reader task** | Understand the invariant sequence surrounding all three interfaces and why every Crossing consequence begins after complete passage rather than interrupting the Roadway |
| **Augment form** | Designed prose-and-reference page with one editable five-step sequence, a worked Squirrel resolution ending in a far-side TRAVEL tableau, a compact Flyer band, and an Automatic Return/Full RISK comparison |
| **Working subject** | **Commit, Cross, Resolve** |
| **Scale** | One autonomous landscape page; diagrams and reference fields carry it without another complete interface mockup or dramatic illustration |
| **Narrative function** | Complete the Gale–Alder–Reed Crossing while preserving the Rabbit and Mouse pages as provisional interface demonstrations |
| **Narrative treatment** | Worked play sequence. Gale, Alder, and Reed Commit the plan established in `AUG-5.3-03`, complete one uninterrupted Run, and reach the far side. A brief reverse-angle Staging Post tableau within TRAVEL shows their arrival before direct movement resumes. The selected example resolves without an additional visible personal consequence; each Citizen's first-RISK Exposure floor becomes part of hidden continuing state and remains unavailable to the interface |
| **Register** | RISK resolution returning to TRAVEL, with homeward procedure shown as reference only |
| **Subjects** | Gale, Alder, Reed, the same Elmwater Roadway, and general Rabbit, Mouse, Squirrel, Crow, and Gull reference figures where required |
| **Incoming state** | The Squirrel screen holds one complete legal passage at the final Commit boundary |
| **Outgoing state** | The far-side TRAVEL tableau has shown the whole party safely beyond traffic and applied the bounded result; the shared group passage is complete; hidden Exposure is at least 1 for each Citizen; direct movement resumes from the actual far-side position |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 5.3; Chapter 5.6 first-RISK Exposure floor; Appendix F Road Work effects; Appendix G Crossing, Flyer, adversity, and Return Crossing procedure |
| **Semantic content** | **Plan → Commit → Continuous Run → Resolve → Continue**. Commit ends strategic input; the Roadway remains in motion; no steering, reaction prompt, or mid-road MEET occurs; every committed party reaches the far side; group passage resolves before delay, Exposure, separation, eligible Cargo loss, Wound, Maiming, Tharn, death, or another valid personal effect; one causal result does not receive duplicate charges; adversity follows Body Units. Resolve hands off to a brief far-side TRAVEL tableau that reverses the Staging Post angle, depicts the arrived party, and applies the bounded result before direct movement resumes. It is not a MEET and does not automatically open one. A compact Road Work field shows that changed conditions or penalties apply after the Run rather than trapping the party mid-road. The Flyer band limits the parallel expression to Crow and Gull, preserves shared commitment and time, and resolves launch, River Spume, weather, visibility, and landing separately without bypassing Exposure or consequence. The homeward field distinguishes ordinary **Automatic Return** from exceptional **Full RISK**, with both carrying the whole party to the far side before the same arrival treatment applies any penalty |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable diagrams and small current-spec derivative frames from `AUG-5.3-03`; the far-side tableau remakes the same Staging Post from the reverse side rather than commissioning another independent Crossing scene |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 2.5, 5.6, and 5.8; Appendices F and G; all three Core RISK mockups |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved; exact far-side continuation and any visibly narrated clean result remain for the coordinated Part V sequence brief |
| **Acceptance test** | The shared grammar unifies without flattening the three interfaces; Commit is visibly irreversible; nobody is stranded mid-Roadway; consequence begins in the far-side TRAVEL tableau rather than a new MEET; Mouse headcount does not multiply adversity; only Crow and Gull receive Flyer treatment; Automatic Return cannot be mistaken for teleportation or immunity; and hidden Exposure remains absent from the literal interface |

The Chapter therefore demonstrates rather than merely asserts the Species
difference:

> **Rabbit waits for an opening. Mouse makes continuity. Squirrel composes
> motion.**

This three-complete-interface treatment is unique to RISK. Crossing is the one
Register whose operative presentation changes fundamentally with the Core
Species. Other Chapters may compare Species effects, bodies, civilizations,
or affordances, but they do not receive three complete parallel interface
mockups merely to maintain representational symmetry.

#### 5.4 Nodes in the Field

| **Working subject** | **A Place Before It Becomes a Situation** |
| **Scale** | One autonomous landscape page; one dominant Node and restrained supporting insets rather than a multi-page sequence |
| **Narrative function** | Continue Gale, Alder, and Reed from their first Crossing to the location that will own Chapter 5.5's MEET |
| **Narrative treatment** | Recurring lived moment and narrative-bearing environment. The party approaches and recognizes the Node; the page ends when its actual present condition crosses the MEET threshold, before any response is selected |
| **Register** | TRAVEL approaching contextual MEET |
| **Subjects** | Gale, Alder, Reed, and one water-affected Margin Node on the far side of the Elmwater Roadway; exact Node identity, present inhabitant or condition, and stakes remain coordinated with Chapter 5.5 |
| **Incoming state** | The party has completed the Crossing resolved in `AUG-5.3-04`, stands on the Margin side, and carries the hidden first-RISK Exposure floor without another visible consequence |

| **Semantic content** | One stable Node ground and identity with visible material, ecology, shelter, infrastructure, inhabitants or traces, prior alteration, and history. Four subordinate insets show possible relationships to that same place: **quiet observation remains TRAVEL; brief uncontested use may resolve within the current Band; genuinely occupying work advances the next Band; meaningful danger, opposition, change, or opportunity opens MEET**. Approach, visibility, opposition, renewal, season, weather, human activity, and prior events alter the present situation without changing the place into another Node type. Hazard is one current condition or present animal; remembered events attach to the Node's history. Reed's body and Signature may become relevant after MEET frames the situation but grant no passive TRAVEL, revelation, collection, or access benefit on this page. Material left on arbitrary ground leaves play unless an authored Node change makes it part of this persistent place |

| **Approval state** | Approved; exact Node, present condition or counterpart, known history, stakes, and MEET threshold remain to be authored jointly with Chapter 5.5 |
| **Acceptance test** | One place remains recognizable through every inset; the four relationships cannot be mistaken for formal Node classes; the actual worked state stops before response selection; Reed's Water Reach remains a contextual MEET affordance rather than a passive travel power; Hazard and memory remain properties of present condition and history; no cleared/completed tag, resource-reset promise, arbitrary cache, route, or specialist-Node taxonomy appears |

The page contracts after Chapter 5.3's exceptional four-page treatment. Its
single place carries the transition:

> **The Field reveals a place → present facts make the place consequential →
> MEET gives that consequence a decision.**

#### 5.5 MEET: Consequential Situations

Chapter 1.5 has already taught MEET's anatomy and general temporal procedure.
This Chapter therefore stays with one actual Away situation long enough for
choice to change it. One complete interface and one changed-state page carry
the example; MEET does not receive parallel Core-Species screens.

##### `AUG-5.5-01` — The Current Claim

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 5.5, contested Away MEET, response expressions, Commit, Focus, shared result, and causal personal consequence |

| **Register** | Contested Away MEET arising from TRAVEL at a Node |

At least one family may be visibly inactive only for a concise knowable situated reason. The player provisionally selects the EVADE response that uses the lower waterline. Only then do Alder's applicable Brace Tool and Reed's **REACH — Water Reach** appear. The Tool is selected as the Round's one personal-Item expression; Reed's bodily Signature coexists with it. The player may revise the response or expression until Commit. After Commit, Alder and Reed form the eligible Focus set and one is selected randomly; this worked result gives Focus to Alder without reroll. The party first secures the wire on stable ground. Reed then receives the Wound because Reed physically entered the current and struck a submerged edge; Alder's Focus neither causes nor redirects it. The selected Tool supplies its effect before entering the Round's actual hazard and remains usable in this worked result |
| **Asset lineage** | New current-spec interface using mandatory canon images and a new Node backplate coordinated with Chapters 5.2–5.4. Development MEET screens inform only scene-primary composition; their obsolete meters, response families, portrait strips, advisor copy, and encounter taxonomy do not survive |

| **Acceptance test** | The scene remains primary; the five families remain fixed and contextual; Tool and Signature appear only after response selection; only one personal Item is committed; Focus resolves after Commit; Alder can receive Focus while Reed receives the causally owned Wound; the shared result precedes personal consequence; and the first Round visibly changes rather than completes the situation |

The page should make the distinction unusually legible without turning it into
a warning label:

> **Focus determines whose action the Round follows. Cause determines who
> bears its consequence.**

Together the pages perform the Chapter's full argument through one situation:

> **A choice changes the situation → the changed situation changes the next
> choice → aftermath belongs to the world that produced it.**

This closes the expedition's current active incident rather than its narrative
run. Reed's Wound remains a persistent fact while Chapter 5.6 briefly steps
aside to explain bodily consequence through sourcebook reference and bounded
Story-Colony histories. Chapter 5.7 then resumes the same Morningside party.

#### 5.6 Exposure and Bodily Consequence

Chapter 5.6 suspends the Morningside expedition spine without replacing it.
Its three pages separate hidden system explanation, one embodied Rabbit
history, and one abrupt Mouse crisis. Fennel and Crumb are explicitly bounded
historical cases rather than the next parties in Part V's continuous journey.
The examples illuminate bodily consequence without allowing a Citizen to
become a diagram of a condition.

##### `AUG-5.6-01` — The Hidden Weight of Exposure

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 5.6, hidden Exposure structure, escalation, communication, and recovery |
| **Reader task** | Understand exactly how Exposure accumulates and weights uncertain consequence while recognizing that the theoretical game's player never receives its number or ladder |
| **Augment form** | Information-rich full-page infographic with one sourcebook-only backend structure and a clearly separate player-perception field |
| **Working subject** | **The Hidden Weight of Exposure** |
| **Scale** | One full infographic page within the Chapter |
| **Narrative function** | None; this page establishes the concealed system before named lives demonstrate distinct consequences |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally none. No Story Citizen is used as the specimen for the six accumulated states |
| **Register** | Cross-register bodily-state reference rather than a literal game view |
| **Subjects** | Baseline 0; hidden Exposure states 1–6; one generic causal situation; visible bodily, behavioral, vocal, narrative, and contextual communication; Stopover, Outpost, and Home recovery |
| **Incoming state** | Earlier Part V Chapters have shown first RISK, multiple Day Bands, situated danger, Wound, and persistent party state without exposing an Exposure value |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can reconstruct every Exposure transaction while understanding why no literal interface in the book displays the backend number |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 5.6 and Appendix G Exposure, personal consequence, Tharn, and recovery rules; Chapter 5.3 first-RISK sequence; Chapter 5.7 Stopover and Outpost effects |
| **Semantic content** | A sourcebook-only hidden ladder distinguishes **0 baseline** from accumulated states **1–6**. A Citizen's first RISK sets a floor of 1 after Crossing resolution; an explicit Exposure cause ordinarily adds one; one cause supplies at most one base gain; an earlier unstabilized Wound doubles that normal gain to two; a Wound created by the same incident does not double its linked gain; Exposure caps at 6. When genuine uncertainty remains after situation and causal target are known, **Exposure escalation chance = 10% × current Exposure** may move the result one position toward the serious end of the situation's already-valid ordered range. It neither selects a target, creates a new consequence, repeats for linked effects, nor acts when the result is determined or already most serious. Pre-existing Exposure and Wound do not weight RISK itself; any Exposure awarded by the resolved Crossing then enters persistence. At every state, movement, Carry, choices, Civic Share, control, and aptitude receive no standing penalty. A separate player-perception field uses body, behavior, voice, narration, and context without fixed one-to-one tiers or a hidden-state reveal. Recovery shows an uninterrupted ordinary Stopover removing up to one step, an interrupted Field Stopover removing none, a usable Outpost or Rest-Stop Metropolis Stopover removing up to two, and no Stopover removing the final Away step. Home removes up to three steps per DAWN, including the maximum path **6 → 3 → 0**, but gives Exposure no distinct domestic manifestation: hidden recovery recedes into ordinary DWELL calm |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable infographic. No legacy harm ladder, Fear state, condition meter, exposed probability, fatigue track, status-wheel, or generic distressed Citizen survives as content |
| **Cross-reference** | Chapters 4.1, 5.2, 5.3, 5.5, 5.7, and 5.8; Appendices D and G; `AUG-5.6-02` and `AUG-5.6-03` |
| **Priority** | Essential |
| **Approval state** | Approved; final graphic hierarchy and player-perception examples remain for the asset brief |
| **Acceptance test** | The six accumulated states and baseline are exact; the graphic is unmistakably editorial rather than player-facing; Exposure weights only genuine uncertainty inside an existing range; first RISK timing and Wound doubling are correct; maximum Exposure causes nothing by itself; recovery is exact; and no visible symptom is falsely bound to one numerical state |

##### `AUG-5.6-02` — The Wound Heals; the Maiming Remains

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 5.6, Wound, Maiming, Remedy stabilization, Home care, and continuing bodily identity |
| **Reader task** | Distinguish one acute recoverable Wound from the lasting bodily change produced by the same maiming injury, and understand what Remedy and later care actually accomplish |
| **Augment form** | Designed narrative-and-reference page with one principal present-life portrait, one restrained rescue-history inset, and one editable causal timeline |
| **Working subject** | **The Wound Heals; the Maiming Remains** |
| **Scale** | One autonomous landscape page; Fennel's present life holds more visual weight than the injury event |
| **Narrative function** | Give the rules an embodied Rabbit history while establishing Fennel as a continuing Citizen rather than an example whose purpose ends with rescue |
| **Narrative treatment** | Authored Story Colony history. Shadepatch finds Fennel inside a flooded culvert; Fennel returns with one acute Wound and a lasting hind-leg Maiming. Remedy stabilizes the Wound through Homecoming, and later Hearth-and-Caretaker care clears it while the Maiming remains part of Fennel's visible life |
| **Register** | Historical Away MEET aftermath, Homecoming reference, and present Home life |
| **Subjects** | Fennel; Shadepatch, later called Sharpnose; the flooded culvert; one Remedy; an available Hearth; the resulting Caretaker Balance; Fennel's current adaptive movement and equipment where canonically established |
| **Incoming state** | Fennel is trapped in a flooded culvert when Shadepatch locates the concealed Citizen; neither injury result has yet been resolved |
| **Outgoing state** | Fennel belongs to New Elderbank as a continuing Citizen whose acute Wound has healed and whose hind-leg Maiming, adaptations, relationships, work, and ordinary preferences persist |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 5.6; Chapters 3.7, 4.1, 4.4, 4.6, and 5.8; Appendix D Wound Recovery and Rehabilitation; Appendix E Remedy; Appendix H New Elderbank history; current Fennel and Shadepatch/Sharpnose canon imagery |
| **Semantic content** | The causal timeline reads **maiming incident → one acute named Wound + one lasting Maiming → Remedy stabilization through Homecoming → Body-Unit-scaled Patient Load + one available Hearth for one to three Recovery Days at Home → Wound clears while Maiming remains → adaptation and optional individualized Rehabilitation address lived capability without erasing history**. Wound recovery is ordinary Caretaker work: it enters no Project Queue, consumes no material, and commits no named Caretaker. The Patient Load remains in Caretaker Balance until the Wound clears; an uncovered amount becomes ordinary Caretaker Pressure without pausing an occupied Hearth's recovery clock. Remedy stabilizes rather than heals and contains rather than removes the acute Wound accompanying Maiming. A later Wound would change the one existing Wound description and preserve the greater current or authored recovery requirement rather than stack another Wound. The rescue inset names the rescuer **Shadepatch** because the After-name has not yet been conferred; a restrained later note explains that the Colony's subsequent observance recognizes the act through **Sharpnose**. Fennel's principal image shows current ordinary life, relationship, work, and bodily adaptation without heroicizing pain or restoring an unmaimed body after recovery |
| **Asset lineage** | Reference-and-amend manifest rows 69, 159, and 160 for the flooded-culvert and Sharpnose history only after replacing the obsolete wildcat event, chronology, anatomy, and cast. Fennel requires a current canon-grounded present-life image rather than a generic injured Rabbit plate |

#### 5.7 Outposts and Stopovers

The Chapter resumes Gale, Alder, and Reed after the two historical case
studies. Their Stopover gives Reed's Wound and the expedition's accumulating
state an ordinary, credible reason to use an Outpost. It then steps away from
the party to explain the infrastructure waiting behind that Stopover. A Stopover
is a temporary achieved state; an Outpost is a persistent uninhabited place
that makes one such state reliable.

Stopover supplies no Recovery Days, does not clear Reed's Wound, removes no Maiming, and does not retrospectively change the prior MEET. > **Sleep enters Stopover. An Outpost makes one known Stopover dependable.

#### 5.8 Return and Homecoming

The continuous expedition now turns home. Return remains ordinary movement
through the same world; Homecoming begins only when Gale, Alder, and Reed reach
Morningside itself. The Chapter gives the journey one physical return page and
one state-changing reception page.

#### 5.9 Landscape Voice: The Playable Page

Chapter 5.9 is Part V's presentational coda. The expedition has already ended
at Homecoming; the Chapter returns editorially to an earlier interval of its
TRAVEL rather than adding another event after the party reaches Home. Its
augment reuses the literal scene established for Chapter 5.2 so the reader can
see exactly how one playable state becomes briefly literary without requiring
a second illustration.

##### AUG-5.9-01 — The Playable Page

|---|---|

The principal passage describes current light, ground, vegetation, direction, collective movement, and growing Roadway sound. A five-link trace pairs current facts with narrated attention—for example **late light → lengthening wall-shadow; dry verge vegetation → pale stems; northward direct movement → the party's concrete progress; open ground → bodily spacing; increasing traffic proximity → Roadway sound overcoming smaller sounds**. The trace demonstrates selection and syntax, not a text-generation interface visible to the player. Two subordinate text-only examples contract the cadence near an already depicted Crossing approach and relax it after a simple spatially meaningful resolution; they are alternative earned uses, not three passages that would fire during one short journey. A final boundary line states that Landscape Voice can deepen apprehension of what is present but cannot reveal a hidden hazard, decide an unresolved outcome, supply essential instruction, declare emotion, add a mechanic, or write Campaign Memory. The sequence **terrain → perception → available choices → movement → consequence** remains legible beneath the prose |

This single page carries the Chapter's full augment burden. Appendix B retains
the broader style, influence, cadence, and usage specification; the body needs
one convincing demonstration rather than a second miniature language manual.

#### Development follow-up — the expedition as a vertical slice

The coordinated Gale–Alder–Reed expedition has also proved useful as a
development **vertical slice**: one exact Colony state can be instantiated
before Launch, reduced by the party's real departure, advanced through DAWN
while attention is Away, and reconciled through Homecoming and recovery. The
first numerical exercise established the daily baseline of one Sustenance per
resident Body Unit, including Citizens Away. Its other quantities—including
resource levels in Stores, Practice counts, yields, spoilage, Cargo, Project
duration, and individual rolls—remain hypotheses rather than canon or final
tuning.

The complete narrative, Story-Canon context, current-baseline ledger, and
earlier procedural transcript are preserved in
[`M050_Gale_Expedition_Vertical_Slice.md`](M050_Gale_Expedition_Vertical_Slice.md).

Return to this slice as a whole before producing the coordinated Part V asset
brief. Its value is diagnostic: Role Capacity and Load, Civic Shares, passive
production, Sustenance, Carry, Cargo, Supplies, Tools, RISK, MEET, Outpost
transfer, Homecoming, care, and Campaign Memory can all be checked against one
continuous set of named lives and one ledger. It should test whether the game
holds together, not force the Sourcebook to print a complete accounting log.

### 6.7 Part VI — The Campaign

Part VI widens the timescale from one expedition to the history accumulated by
a continuing Colony. Its opening should make duration perceptible before
explaining how the game stores or presents it. Because Part V has followed
Morningside closely, this threshold returns the book decisively to Mouse life
and lets Rushbottom carry the first campaign-scale image.

Chapter 6.1 first establishes authority and information flow, then lets the
reader inspect one event in its actual player-facing forms. The two tasks stay
on separate self-sufficient pages: the first prevents Chronicle, Tales, canon
images, and Almanac from becoming a decorative collage; the second prevents
the architecture from remaining an abstract database diagram.

#### 6.2 The opening campaign

Chapter 6.2 explains how the game authors its beginning without retelling Part
0. The Rabbit Founding Escape supplies the principal visual continuity, while
the mechanical account remains true for the selected Core Species and makes
the Mouse opening exception exact. The augment sequence separates four
questions: what remains playable during the escape, how ground becomes Home,
how the three species reach equal opening scale, and where the tutorial yields
to ordinary campaign choice.

#### Development follow-up — opening-campaign parity test

The opening campaign is a strong future candidate for the paired narrative and
system exploration method. Before its coordinated asset brief or final economy
tuning, instantiate Mouse, Rabbit, and Squirrel openings from Founding through
first Homecoming against the same current rules: one Sustenance per resident
Body Unit per day, Exposure 6 recovery, the fixed reserve, provisional cache,
Housing Pressure, Workshop bootstrap, Residence-or-Garden choice, Role
subtraction at Launch, and ordinary Homecoming reconciliation. Use comparable
player choices where comparison is meaningful without erasing body, party, or
Species differences.

The Mouse opening merits the accompanying narrative realization: four Founders
receiving Weft and Warp, building through half Civic Shares, and sending a
first legal party should reveal whether six individually particular lives feel
like parity rather than multiplication. The ledgers and narrative remain
development evidence until authorial discussion adopts any discovery; no
provisional resource amount, duration, dialogue, or outcome becomes canon by
appearing in the exercise.

#### 6.3 Civic progression

Chapter 6.3 must make civic progression legible without turning the campaign
into an upgrade ladder. Three treatments separate the scale already reached,
the deliberate act by which that scale is recognized, and the other kinds of
persistent development that remain independent of Tier. Mouse, Rabbit, and
Squirrel receive equal visual weight in the comparative page. The sustained
worked history then moves to Morningside: Part VI has opened with Rushbottom
and Chapter 6.2 principally follows New Elderbank, so a Squirrel Recognition
completes the rotation without forcing species symmetry into every page.

##### AUG-6.3-01 — One Scale, Three Civic Forms

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 6.3's four Colony Tiers and population eligibility; Appendix D Tier reference; Appendix H Story Civilizations |
| **Reader task** | Read the four common mechanical scales, their Body-Unit thresholds, and the three Core Species' civic names and physical expressions without interpreting Tier as a population cap, building level, or unlock tree |
| **Augment form** | Full-page comparative infographic with one common four-Tier scale and three equally weighted civilization sequences |
| **Working subject** | **One Scale, Three Civic Forms** — Rushbottom, New Elderbank, and Morningside at the moments their four recognized civic forms become true |
| **Scale** | One complete landscape page; every Tier and Species must remain readable without an adjacent spread |
| **Narrative function** | Long-duration recognition, parity, and difference through accumulated inhabited form |
| **Narrative treatment** | Intentionally no continuous prose. Exact Story-Civilization snapshots make the comparison particular, while concise labels identify them as examples rather than prescribed campaign schedules |
| **Register** | Campaign reference grounded in successive DWELL states, not a literal upgrade interface |
| **Subjects** | Rushbottom as First Rooms, Joined House, Manor House, and Grand Manor; New Elderbank as Close Commons, Open Commons, Court, and Grand Court; Morningside as First Anchors, Linked Ways, Living Web, and Grand Web; their current-canon ground and representative lived populations |
| **Incoming state** | Chapter 6.2 has established all three Colonies at the same three-Body-Unit Founding scale through different numbers of complete individual Citizens |
| **Outgoing state** | The reader can translate a common Tier number or Body-Unit threshold into each Species' own civic language and can recognize Tier IV as optional grand ambition rather than the mandatory end of play |
| **Canon inputs** | Chapter 6.3; Appendix D Colony Tier recognition; Appendix H's exact Story-Civilization Tier snapshots and rosters; current Species spatial grammars; current and superseded Colony canon-image sets |
| **Semantic content** | A common scale establishes **Tier I: Founding**, **Tier II: 7 living Body Units**, **Tier III: 12**, and **Tier IV: 18**. Concise interpretive captions distinguish the founded Home, the Colony able to prepare beyond its next immediate need, the mature and durable Home able to endure and choose, and optional grand ambition; these meanings are recognitions of scale, never additional eligibility tests. The three equal sequences name **First Rooms → Joined House → Manor House → Grand Manor**, **Close Commons → Open Commons → Court → Grand Court**, and **First Anchors → Linked Ways → Living Web → Grand Web**. Story examples show Rushbottom's **6 / 12 / 20 / 30 named Citizen subjects** and New Elderbank's and Morningside's **3 / 7 / 12 / 18 named Citizens** at those recognitions while retaining the shared **3 / 7 / 12 / 18 Body-Unit** scale. Every Mouse remains separately depicted and named; collective-bodied Guests remain single Citizens. A narrow eligibility rail states that every living Roster Citizen contributes their ordinary Body Unit regardless of age, health, availability, or current location and that population alone creates Tier eligibility. The Colony views accumulate inhabited specificity rather than simply becoming larger silhouettes. Tier names describe recognized civic form and historical scale; no stat increase, content unlock, population ceiling, or required roster composition follows from the diagram |
| **Asset lineage** | New editable comparative composition built from mandatory current Colony representations and Citizen references. #### 6.4 Corridor progression

Chapter 6.4 shows a world becoming known, revisitable, and practically
accessible without turning geography into a ladder. Its two pages move from
the campaign-scale map to one particular place across repeated visits. The
first page explains accumulated reach without redrawing TRAVEL or Outpost
mechanics already established in Part V. The second makes persistence lived:
the same ground can hold ecology, material, an agreement, depletion, recovery,
and recognition at once.

##### AUG-6.4-01 — The Same World, More Known

|---|---|

#### Development follow-up — accumulated-Corridor campaign test

Before the coordinated Chapter 6.4 asset brief, use the paired narrative and
mechanical-simulation method to instantiate the three bramble visits and one
intervening Outpost Project from a single exact New Elderbank state. Track the
actual Home subtraction, parties, Day Bands, Crossings, travel cadence,
Exposure, Carry and Cargo, Node abundance, relationship facts, returns, Home
reconciliation, Builder commitment, and later practical range. The exercise
should test whether the world feels progressively familiar without acquiring
Routes or a progression score, and whether the map and Almanac can present the
result without duplicating Campaign Memory.

The simulation is diagnostic evidence. Resource quantities, Node yields,
recovery intervals, Project requirements, exact dialogue, and MEET outcomes
remain provisional until separately adopted; appearance in the ledger or
narrative does not make them canonical tuning.

#### 6.5 Landmark Reaches in the Campaign

Chapter 6.5 gives the campaign's farthest distinctive territories their own
civic and emotional identities without converting them into a mandatory
sequence of special levels. Its full-page comparison serves as the Chapter
opening, so no separate decorative Chapter plate is required. A Remembrance
history then returns the Rabbit book-spine to the Sourcebook's illustrative
Elderbank, while a literal comparative page shows why generated Interchange
Expanses and Rest-Stop Metropolis both remain Away territories despite their
radically different forms of plurality.

The complete Story-Civilization horizontal slices remain in Appendix H. Core
uses recurring Citizens and bounded excerpts from those histories where a
specific horizon becomes lived play; it does not repeat all three Tier
chronologies at the campaign climax.

##### AUG-6.5-01 — Three Landmark Reaches

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|
| **GDD location** | Chapter 6.5's Ancestral Home, Interchange Expanses, Rest-Stop Metropolis, ordinary Away access, Landmark Reach identity, continuing availability, and absence of a formal victory condition |

| **Semantic content** | Part II first establishes the world-body distinction: an ordinary Interchange biome retains one Median, two Main Roadways, and two Margins beneath interchange infrastructure, while each Landmark Reach gives a whole Reach exceptional geography and a composed Node slate without becoming a separate map or progression tier. Every Landmark Reach remains persistent Field territory reached at whatever current practical range permits and uses ordinary direct TRAVEL, RISK, Nodes, MEET, relationships, bodily consequence, Stopover, Return, and Campaign Memory. **Ancestral Home — generated society lost:** the Reach appears once at its opening-established position. Interchange Expanse-like multi-Roadway TRAVEL geometry carries specialist remains derived from the opening Colony, authored story Nodes bound to compatible campaign particulars, possible Remembrance, and the low-frequency possibility of one lone escapee. It has no ruin-completion score, restoration path, or second Home. **Interchange Expanse — renewable abundance and plurality in immediate circumstance:** the Landmark Reach may autogenerate elsewhere in the Corridor. Formal Margins disappear at its longitudinal Reach-border Crossing without requiring explanation. Ground-level Roadways divide playable ground and require ordinary Crossing; elevated Roadways establish the Landmark Reach in vistas and backplates while remaining decorative; below-grade Roadways may become impassable interior boundaries. Many renewable Resource Nodes and a smaller authored situation set support varied animals, local claims, unusual material, camps, exchange, danger, and repeated visits. **Rest-Stop Metropolis — plurality gathered into a lasting city:** stable markets, shelters, workshops, gathering places, specialist venues, substantial Stopover, trade, craft, information, hospitality, and situated Practice-improvement understanding. Unique authored service Nodes include Tool repair and trade, Resource barter, **3 Perishable Sustenance → 2 Durable Sustenance** preservation, and a Metropolis-only Scrap transformer offering **3 Flexible Scrap → 2 Rigid Scrap** or **3 Rigid Scrap → 2 Flexible Scrap**. Any city service duplicated at Home costs its ordinary Colony recipe plus one additional recipe-relevant Resource chosen by the venue. The Metropolis remains navigated and participated in rather than managed through DWELL. None is tied to Colony Tier, no order is mandatory, the Corridor continues beyond them, and none constitutes victory or campaign completion |
| **Asset lineage** | Three current-spec environmental remakes. The illustrative Ancestral image must derive from the exact Part 0 Elderbank representation and show the same ground after destruction rather than a generic ruin; this visual continuity demonstrates campaign generation and does not install Elderbank in the game. Manifest rows 230, 242, and 244 may inform Interchange Expanse breadth, multiple Roadway levels, scattered infrastructure, and node-rich ground. Rest-Stop Metropolis rows 62, 98, and 156 may inform density, venue legibility, and market atmosphere only after obsolete society, scale, cast, labels, and mechanics are removed. Every heading and instructional distinction remains production typography |

| **Priority** | Essential |

| **Acceptance test** | The old place is unmistakably the location shown intact in Part 0 and its multi-Roadway Landmark-Reach geography does not turn it into an Interchange Expanse; the page identifies Elderbank and every named subject as Sourcebook illustration rather than fixed game content; Landscape Voice knows only the present world; Bramble remembers, Daisy inherits, and Burdock witnesses without their perspectives collapsing; the MEET contains real choices supported by the site; no stale Law is quoted; no object is invented merely to create loot; the Keepsake remains governed by Item rules; the lone escapee is neither promised nor foreshadowed; the party remains Away; and the event deepens New Elderbank rather than replacing it |

##### AUG-6.5-03 — Crossroads and City

| Field | Approved mapping |
|---|---|

No transaction or relationship is resolved, so the comparison does not fix a new event in Rushbottom history. The same recognizable party supplies bodily scale and continuity across both hypothetical visits |

| **Subjects** | Bran and Nook as illustrative Mouse Citizens; Rill as illustrative Weasel Expedition Guest; one generated Interchange Expanse's ground-level Roadways, decorative elevated infrastructure, possible below-grade boundary, ramps, pillars, shelters, renewable Resources, temporary camps, claims, and varied animals; Metropolis streets or passages, stable specialist venues, residents, visitors, Tool repair and trade, Resource barter, food preservation, Scrap transformation, and substantial Stopover |
